This is what an agricultural planet looks like from orbit: the continental surface is divided into huge, regular color blocks—gold (wheat/grain), dark green (forest carbon sinks), light green (vegetable areas), and blue (artificially irrigated lakes/aquaculture). The boundaries of the color blocks are straight lines and curves, so precise they seem unnatural. The entire planet looks like a coloring page.
On the ground: endless wheat fields stretch to the horizon. No fences—no animals to contain (the CI on the agricultural planet typically avoids large animals—adding complexity but not efficiency). Unmanned harvesters move like schools of fish through the wheat, each maintaining precise spacing. The air smells like dry grain and sun-warmed soil—similar to the smell in Archeon's Basin Seven fields, but Archeon's version has an added layer of human and spiky sheep scents; the agricultural planet's version is unrealistically clean.
The only "city" is the harvesting and processing center—a massive silo and processing facility operated by a small number of mixed-sex administrators (who choose to reside in physical bodies because CI sometimes requires human intuition to handle anomalies) and a large number of unmanned systems. Next to the center is a small residential area, perhaps for 200-500 people, and it is the only place on the entire planet with "streets" and "windows".

§F4 Federation Fleet: A 31st-Century Physics Experience
F4.1 Mag-Bond Armor
The standard spacecraft armor works by rearranging the molecular bonds of the material at the nanoscale using a directional magnetic field, making it 5-10 times harder than diamond. This isn't simply "adding a hard shell"—it's altering the material's internal structure. The same alloy plate is an ordinary metal when the magnetic field is off; when the magnetic field is on, the molecular bonds are strengthened to a near-unbreakable state.
Sensory Details: The magnetically reinforced armor exhibits several perceptible characteristics when activated—its surface temperature is 2-3 degrees Celsius higher than the environment (maintaining the magnetic field requires energy, and waste heat is conducted through the shell). A very subtle vibration can be felt when the palm is placed on it—the operating frequency of the magnetic field generator, approximately 40-60 Hz, is below the auditory threshold but above the tactile threshold. Under specific lighting angles, the armor surface reveals fine textures—stress lines created by molecular rearrangement, resembling the annual rings of wood, but on the micrometer scale. These textures occasionally produce a faint interference color under sunlight—not a rainbow, but more like an invisible oil film floating on the steel surface.
Why is this armor needed? Not for war. The everyday hazards of space travel—micrometeorites (particles impacting at speeds of tens of kilometers per second), high-energy cosmic rays (amplified by blueshift, especially during warp drive), and asteroid debris from colonies (galaxies without a Jupiter-like gravitational sweeper face a constant threat of small celestial bodies)—require all manned spacecraft to have a shell strength several orders of magnitude greater than that of ordinary materials.
The narrative contrast of armor: The Archeon airship's "armor" is riveted copper plates and canvas coverings on a wooden deck—each patch a memory of a repair. The Federation's Mag-Bond armor is seamless, mathematically perfect, and warm to the touch—every inch of surface is the same. If Anna were to touch the Federation's hull one day (perhaps far in the future after Ep69), she would find it unlike a ship—too perfect, so perfect that fingers accustomed to welds and rivets wouldn't know how to "read" it.
F4.2 Flagship-class engineering vessel (~5km)
The entire Federation may consist of only 4-6 ships. Each ship is a mobile industrial city. Design objectives: planetary defense (asteroid orbit deflection) and terraform engineering (atmospheric terraforming delivery, crustal structure modification, and orbital infrastructure deployment).
Antimatter projectile delivery system: The primary tool for Terraform operations. The engineering vessel carries industrial-grade antimatter production facilities (particle accelerator + magnetic confinement storage array), capable of producing and storing up to several hundred kilograms of antimatter on-site at the work site.
The standard procedure for artificially creating plate boundaries is as follows: CI calculates the target fault line (based on a planetary geological model—crust thickness, rock type, and deep heat flow distribution) → deep wells are drilled every tens of kilometers along the fault line (by advance unmanned drilling rigs) → micro antimatter projectiles (each weighing tens to hundreds of grams and encased in a magnetically confined shell) are projected into the wells by an electromagnetic accelerator → synchronous detonation.
The visual effects of the detonation: From orbit, a white flash appears simultaneously every tens of kilometers along the fracture line. Each flash lasts about 0.1 seconds—the gamma-ray burst from antimatter annihilation is absorbed by the rock, converting into heat and shock waves. After the flashes disappear, red begins to appear along the fracture line—magma gushes out from the cracks. The entire process, from detonation to the visible magma, takes several minutes to several hours (depending on the thickness of the Earth's crust).
From the planet's surface (if someone is standing at a safe distance): the ground tremors are roughly equivalent to a magnitude 6-7 earthquake. There's a low-hanging flash on the horizon—not like lightning, but more like an extremely rapid sunrise and sunset. A few hours later, the sky in that direction begins to redden—atmospheric disturbances caused by the light and heat of rising magma.
Energy scale: 100 kg of antimatter + 100 kg of matter annihilation = approximately 1.8 × 10¹⁹ joules = approximately 4300 megatons of TNT equivalent = approximately 86,000 Tsar Bombs. Distributed along a fracture line thousands of kilometers long, each point receives an energy equivalent to hundreds of Tsar Bombs. Enough to crack a planet's crust but not enough to shatter it—this is precision engineering, not destruction.
Recoil issue: The antimatter projectile has a mass in the hundreds of grams range, and the electromagnetic accelerator's projection speed is approximately tens of km/s. The recoil force is practically zero for an engineering vessel at a distance of 5 km and a mass in the hundreds of millions of tons. The real engineering challenge is radiation management—the gamma-ray bursts produced by annihilation generate secondary particle rain in the atmosphere, and the engineering vessel needs to operate at a safe orbital altitude (hundreds of kilometers or more). Mag-Bond armor is precisely designed to shield against this echo radiation.
F4.3 Conventional Spacecraft
The range is from 100 meters to 2 kilometers. Most of them are: exploration ships (long-range spacecraft carrying scientific research equipment and a small number of personnel), transport ships (shuttles for colonization supplies and personnel), and engineering support ships (flagship-class escort ships carrying specialized equipment).
Notes for sensory comparison:
The Federation spaceship Archeon features seamless Mag-Bond armor on its surface, slightly warm to the touch, with tactile vibrations. Riveted copper plates and canvas skin are hot or cold to the touch, depending on the weather. Sound is almost silent (electromagnetic propulsion), occasionally punctuated by the low-frequency hum of the magnetic field generator, the sputtering of the engine steam, the flapping of the canvas, and the creaking of ropes in pulleys. There is no smell—completely filtered recirculated air—no steam, lubricating oil, wood, sweat, or sometimes the smell of cargo from the cargo hold. Control is AI-assisted or fully automated; human input is for high-level decision-making, not manual operation. Anna's hands are on the joysticks; wind changes are transmitted to her palms. The nano-repair system handles maintenance automatically; no human intervention is required. Anna lies in the engine compartment, tightening bolts with a wrench.

§F5 Ep7 Line B Scene Seed: Sofia Sees the Light
Time: Approximately 3010 years. Betelgeuse (the four celestial bodies) arrives on Earth.
Sofia chose to observe with her physical body. She could directly access the observatory's sensor array with her consciousness—obtaining a complete spectral analysis with millisecond-level latency. Her neural interface warmed slightly behind her ear, indicating that data packets were waiting to be received.
She didn't open it.
She went out onto the balcony. The fourth floor of a curved building. The building's biofilm shimmered with a warm orange hue in the twilight. The air smelled—undetectable. The air of the 31st century was too clean; no scent of flowers, soil, or food could penetrate the atmospheric purification system. Sofia sometimes missed scents—she'd read about "the smell of earth after rain" in an old book, something called petrichor. She'd smelled a synthetic version once. It wasn't the same.
The sky. On Orion's shoulder—the star that should have been dark red—was now a mass of white. Not a warm white—but the oversaturated white that made the eyes instinctively constrict their pupils. Her bio-enhanced retina automatically adjusted its iris. Tears welled up—not of emotion, but of brightness.
Several other people came out as well. One of them had a light pattern of data streaming across his eyeballs—he was seeing with his naked eye while simultaneously receiving and analyzing data through a neural interface. Sofia noticed his lips twitch—it was unclear whether it was a reaction to the data or the light.
Inside the hive—if Sofia were willing to "listen"—billions of digital consciousnesses were discussing the matter at a speed she couldn't perceive. She chose not to listen.
The light from a star that died 510 years ago has finally arrived.
Sofia's thoughts: "We still have people near that star."
This sentence would be parsed as an information query within the Hive. In Sofia's words, it's a bodily intuition—500 years ago, a group of people like her who had chosen physical bodies were sent to that star. Then communication was lost.
Narrative Function: On the same day, 520 light-years away, Anna looked up at the same nebula—with two naked eyes that had never been enhanced since birth. Anna had no choice—she only had naked eyes. Sofia had countless choices, but she chose the same way as Anna.



#### Quick Reference for Sensory Comparison

| Dimension | Federation Spaceship | Archeon Airship |
|------|---------------|-------------|
| Surface | Seamless Mag-Bond armor, slightly warm, tactile vibration | Riveted copper plate canvas skin, temperature depends on weather |
| Sound | Nearly silent, occasionally accompanied by a low-frequency hum of the magnetic field | Steam hissing, canvas flapping, rope creaking |
| Odor | None – Fully filtered recirculated air | Steam, lubricating oil, wood, sweat |
| Control | AI-assisted/fully automated, human input is for high-level decision-making | Anna's hands are on the joysticks, the wind travels through the joysticks to her palms |
| Repair | Nano-repair automated processing | Anna lies in the engine compartment tightening bolts with a wrench |

---

### 3.8 Arc 2 Culture Side Module (Atheria/Iris Hollow Homecoming Daily Life Expansion)

Arc 2 (Episodes 20-28) focuses on Anna's identity and belonging, and the nine episodes explore the cultural fabric of the setting. The following subplots, each 500-1500 words long, will not interrupt the main storyline but will simultaneously advance the characters' development.

**A. Story Night – Archeon's Understanding of Earth**

Embedded in the atmosphere of an evening in Athens. A semi-professional storyteller tells children and young people "old stories"—about CI, "the people from the sky" (Atwood Generation), and "further places" (Earth). 521 years of transmission have distorted the stories: "a planet where everyone is sleeping," "houses there grow on their own," "their skies have no nebulae." Some details are remarkably accurate (Atwood ledger was copied many times), while others are completely off-topic (misinformation).

The child asked, "Was the old sound good or bad?" The storyteller replied, "It did what it was supposed to do, and then it left. That's the most dignified way to say goodbye."

Character function: If Lia were present, she might ask, "Then why don't they come back?"—a question almost no one in Archeon asks after 521 years. This plants a remote seed for Anna-Lia's subsequent dialogue about the "impossible."

**B. Proving Day – Education System Showcase**

The daytime segments of Atheria or Iris Hollow. Blacksmith apprentices forge standard parts in front of their masters and fellow apprentices; flight apprentices perform mooring operations. The criteria for judgment are not just "doing it right"—but also "where their attention was focused during the process." Masters may deliberately create distractions to see if the apprentices are distracted.

Archeon had no "school"—he entered the workshop at the age of 13 and followed a master. His knowledge was divided into three layers: CI etching plate ("sacred text") → handwritten copy (simplified + practical notes) → oral transmission from master to apprentice ("Hearing this sound means the temperature has reached").

Character Function: Directly reflects Lia's predicament. At 13, other children entered the workshop, but Lia didn't. When Anna was watching the assessment, she caught a glimpse of Lia standing outside the crowd—not being ostracized, but simply standing there, watching with quiet attention. This visualization of the predicament of "empty hands" doesn't need to be spoken aloud by anyone.

**C. Grey-Throat Migration – A Seasonal Natural Event**

The seasons change in Atheria. Cliff-dwelling gray finches take flight, thousands forming flowing clouds in the canyon, their calls overlapping into a rhythmic buzz. For the people of Atheria, this is the signal of the changing season—"The gray finches are gone, the cool season is coming." Pilots observe flocks of birds differently: they observe hydrodynamics, how they utilize updrafts and change formations at cliff bends.

Role function: Anna points out to Milo the birds splitting and regrouping at the convex corner—"They don't cross the turbulence, they go around it. Just like we do." If Lia were there, she might have noticed a straggling gray finch catching up with the group via a different route. She doesn't speak. But we know what she saw.

**D. Channel Day – A Public Labor Tradition**

The Atheria irrigation system undergoes regular collective maintenance—dredging, gasket replacement, and impeller inspection. The entire valley participates; it's a community activity, not a punishment. Residents from each floor line up along the canal from top to bottom, working and gossiping as they go. Some sing work songs to the rhythm of the shovels. Repair days are also a window for information exchange—news brought by the airship is disseminated a second time.

Role Function: Anna's role changes from "local resident" to "shuttleman and messenger"—she's stopped by all sorts of people asking how things are in the south. Milo participates for the first time, only to have an eight-year-old local child demonstrate the correct shoveling technique in two seconds, while also rolling his eyes. Dignity and humor are activated simultaneously.

**E. Fragment Reading – Knowledge Transfer**

The Frontier City or Iris Hollow Archives. The metal etched plates from the CI period are Archeon's closest thing to being "holy relics"—but not enshrined; they are living references. Dedicated "plate readers" can translate the technical annotations from the CI era. Some plates contain content beyond current comprehension (quantum mechanics, etc.), but have been preserved intact: "Perhaps one day future generations will be able to understand them."

Character Function: Anna helps Lia look up the specifications of an alloy formula. Lia's reaction to the incomprehensible symbols on the etched plate—running her fingers over the engravings—is the same as Anna's way of touching the machine to "listen." If she could read a certain line, it would explain why her lungs are bad. She can't read it. But that gesture connects to the Core projection in Episode 69—a hand placed on it, and so on.

## §4 Character Parameters

### Anna Freedman

| Parameter | Value |
|------|------|
| Full Name | Anna Freedman |
| Gender | Female |
| Age at the start of the story | 19 years old |
Height/Weight | 168cm / 59kg |
| Physique | Lean and muscular, with endurance-type muscle definition sculpted from years of climbing and mechanical labor |
| Skin tone | Light tan, arms and face are one shade darker than the areas being covered |
| Hair | Long, golden hair, usually tied loosely at the back of the head with a leather cord; tucked into the collar or styled in a bun when flying or working |
| Eyes | Blue/grayish-blue, appears grayish under warm lighting in Archeon, leans towards blue in sunlight |
| Face | The facial contours are somewhat sharp, with a clearly defined jawline; subtle fine lines are visible at the corners of the eyes. |
| Hands | The most prominent physical feature – extremely high dexterity |
| Signature Attire | White shirt + dark red short jacket (with reinforced shoulder buckles) + red narrow neck scarf + red short skirt (work apron) + gray trousers + distressed boots |

[Clothing Origin Distinction] Red has two origins:
Jacket = Made by Edmund (Anna, age 10, M-16). Same batch of red clay dye. Edmund's design.
The short skirt/work apron was made by Anna herself (around age 12, during Edmund's decline). She used leftover red cloth from the same batch and tied it around her waist to cover oil stains. Anna was unaware that she was extending Edmund's design.
The origin differences between the two red outfits are not explicitly distinguished in the prose—they are perceived by us as a single, unified whole of "the red on Anna's body."
| Equipment | Fingerless leather gloves, multi-loop tool belt, chest and abdomen cross harness buckle, goggles (usually pushed up to the forehead) |
| Scent | A faint scent of engine oil, metallic dust, and half a bottle of wildflower oil in the side pocket of the flight bag |
| Posture | When standing, the center of gravity is low, and there is a habitual scanning of the environment; when listening, the head is slightly tilted; when thinking, the fingers move unconsciously, as if touching a non-existent part. |
| Voice | Mid-to-low range, speaking speed is relatively fast, and pronunciation is clear; the voice decreases rather than increases when nervous or focused. |
| Father | Edmund Freedman, mechanic, deceased (Anna died when she was 13) |
| Mother | Miriam Freedman, logistics coordinator, currently residing in Eagle's Nest |
| Residence | The Eyrie, located on the upper rock face of the outer rim of Atheria Canyon, at an altitude of approximately 400 meters |
| Occupation | Airship pilot + mechanic (shuttleman), piloting the Class II airship "Rafale" |
| Core Competency | Auditory Diagnosis – Determining Machine Status, Fault Location, and Inter-component Relationships Through Sound |
| Core Weakness | A tendency to do everything personally – distrusting others' ability to do things well and preferring to shoulder all responsibility alone |
| Core Mechanism | "Three-second pause"—Self-examination before making a decision: "Is the hand that pushed me the wind or myself?" (Established after the 13-year-old Lia incident) |

**[Personality – Let Actions Speak for Themselves]**

Anna wasn't "brave"—she was the kind of person whose hands remained steady even in a storm, not because she wasn't afraid, but because her hands were too busy doing something to be afraid. Anna wasn't "clever"—she was the kind of person who would squat next to a stopped water pump, put her hand on it, shut up, wait thirty seconds, and then say, "The second bearing from the left." It wasn't a guess—she heard it.

When she laughs, her whole face moves. She smiles when she's flying high in the sky—not because of the scenery, but because there's a perfect relationship between her body and the wind, a relationship that brings her joy.

Her biggest problem was not believing that others could do what she did. It wasn't arrogance—it was fear. The thought of letting go, of things not going well, of someone getting hurt—was more terrifying than any storm. So she carried everything herself. After the Lia incident when she was 13, she installed a built-in brake in herself: a "three-second pause." Whenever she felt an impulse pushing her forward, she would stop and ask herself: Is the hand pushing me the wind or myself? If it's the wind—go with it. If it's myself—wait.

[M-27 Three-Second Pause Episode Penetration Table]
This mechanism acts like a thread running through the entire story. Each episode receives a slice of itself.
The reader will only have enough information to piece together the whole picture in episodes 45-46.
;
; -- Unexplained behavior (Arc 1) --
Episodes 12-15: Our first encounter with Anna. We only see the behavioral quirk—"Why does she sometimes pause for three seconds?" Zero explanation.
Ep14: Anna looks at the Riverbend fishmonger's hand—"pauses for a second." A second. Then she moves on. The pause is very short because the object is someone else's hand.
;
; ── Externalized as the discipline of apprentices (Arc 2) ──
Ep21 Seed Planting: Anna presses down on Milo's wrist—"Check first, then release. Always the order."
We only see the "strict mentor." The real story: What Anna didn't do on the day of the Lia incident.
;
; ── As part of competition (Arc 2) ──
Ep25: Anna uses her "perceptual system, three-second pause, and red jacket" to fly in the Founder Day Race. The pause here equals competence; we don't know the underlying trauma. Milo Origin: Anna notices Milo's instinct to "wait." After the race, Milo leaves the group for Thornfield.
Ep26: Flight of Faith (Anna solo). The old man (Ep27 Iris Hollow) observes Anna's attention shift from the ground to the sky. The pause extends from her "hand" to the entire direction of her perception—
She didn't just hesitate with her hands; her whole being retreated from close range to a greater distance.
;
; ── Activation when a pause encounters a Lia (Arc 2-3) ──
Episode 33: Anna sees Lia in the Frontier. She takes three steps toward her. Then she stops.
★ The present-tense technique, with its three-second pause, was first used on Lia. No explanation needed.
; Ep35: Flipping through Lia's medical record, on a certain page, there's a three-second pause. Then, "But I can fly."
The fear layer (what if someone gets hurt again?) and the utility layer (I can help her) are offset against each other.
; Ep36: Anna tells Lia that when flying, there is **no need to pause for three seconds**—the content is forward-looking.
Proof by absence: When the pause disappears, the relationship flows smoothly.
;
; ── Lia observes a pause from the outside (Arc 3) ──
Ep42-43: Lia: "Your hand pauses between each step. Not because you don't know what the next step is. It's because you're waiting for something."
Anna: "Waiting for confirmation." Lia: "Confirm what?" Anna: "Confirm that there were no errors in the previous step."
Lia translates the internal mechanism into language. She may know the origins—but she doesn't push Anna's steps forward.
Leverage thinking: Know where the fulcrum is, but don't press down. Offer the answer to the edge and wait for Anna to come to you.
;
; -- Origin Revealed (Arc 3 Climax) --
Ep45: M-26's complete memories unfold. M-27 crystallizes amidst the cracking sounds of glaciers.
The Reader first obtains the complete causal chain: wrench → Lia → three-second pause → perception from near to far.
Ep46: The extreme version of the pause = freeze. Anna is completely frozen above the glacier.
The consequences of "distrusting first instincts" in life-or-death situations. Savona witnessing.
;
; ── The qualitative change of pause (Arc 3 aftermath) ──
Ep48: Anna descends the mountain. Lia notices a change in texture during a pause—"Before, it was a mechanical brake. Now it's heavier."
It's like something new has been added to a pause, but Anna hasn't had time to put it away yet.
Ep49: "You're stopping again. You stop even longer every time you're next to me." The wall goes from invisible to visible.
Ep50: Creed Crystallization. The pause shifts from fear-driven to respect-driven. The M-27 trauma version begins its return to the M-06 origin version.
We haven't arrived yet—but the direction has changed.
;
; -- Low-level rewriting (Arc 4 climax) --
Ep61: Anna paused—"Not a three-second pause—deeper." Three-hit cascade.
After aligning the radiation timeline, the pause at the bottom was rewritten from "My hand hurt someone" to "My father protected my childhood with silence".
;
[End of pass-through table]

Her relationship with her mother, Miriam, was warm but distant. Not cold—it was that both were very independent people who processed Edmund's departure in different ways. Miriam filled her time with busyness, while Anna filled it with work. Occasionally, when they were both in the kitchen, they would eat together quietly, not saying much, but that quiet was comfortable.

### Edmund Freedman

| Parameter | Value |
|------|------|
| Gender | Male |
| Identity | Anna's father, Atheria Valley guardian mechanic |
Status | Deceased, Anna passed away at the age of 13 |
| Method of Presence | Through Anna's memories, the tools and logbook in the workshop, and the ways she perceives things in her body |

**[Personality – Let Actions Speak for Themselves]**

Edmund wasn't a "gentle father"—he was the kind of person who would pull half a biscuit from his pocket and hand it to you without looking at you, because he was listening to the water pump, and the gesture was as natural as breathing. But he was also the kind of person who, after Anna said something about observation, would suddenly stop what he was doing, his eyes lighting up with **joy**. The kind of joy a mechanic feels when he discovers his child has seen what he has seen.

He wasn't a "good teacher"—he was the kind of person who would see Anna already lying on the rock listening to the river and then go over and squat down next to her. Sometimes he wouldn't say anything, just wait for her to finish listening. Sometimes he would lie down himself, their foreheads almost touching, pressed against the same rock, and when Anna looked up, she would find him smiling—not because there was anything funny about the rock, but because the very act of his daughter lying on the ground listening to the rock made him happy.

His way of teaching Anna wasn't through lectures. It was through doing—and then waiting for her to notice. When Anna was observing something, he wouldn't interrupt. When she asked a question, he often wouldn't answer directly, but instead ask a smaller question to lead her to the answer herself. But when she did—when she spoke the answer herself—Edmund's reaction wasn't just confirmation. It was a shared delight. Sometimes he would say nothing but pat her on the back of the head. Sometimes they would look at each other and laugh simultaneously because the answer was so beautiful.

Edmund's happiness wasn't dramatic. It wasn't a "I'm proud of you" declaration. It was a warm current beneath the surface—every time Anna discovered something new, Edmund's world expanded a little more. It was a two-way street. Anna thought Edmund was teaching her how to see the world. In reality, Edmund was also seeing the world anew through Anna—rediscovering the canyon he had known for twenty years through a child's fresh perception.

**Edmund's Guide to Dialogue Variety:** Edmund isn't taciturn to the point of only uttering a single word. He's someone who doesn't waste words, but every sentence he speaks is different. His responses vary depending on the scene, mood, and the person he's interacting with—sometimes a single word, sometimes an action, sometimes something unexpected for Anna, sometimes a question. **When writing, it's strictly forbidden for Edmund to repeat the same word or reaction pattern in different scenes.** If he nodded in the previous scene, he cannot nod in the next. If he used an action in the previous scene, he must give her an actual sentence in the next. Edmund's brevity is varied brevity—each time short, but each time different. This is the quality of him as a living person, not a template for a "silent character."

He was also a builder of new things—not just a repairman. The windbreak wall of the Eagle's Nest workshop, the improved waterwheel, and The Sail kite he made for Anna—were all designed and built by him. He believed that "the greater skill is to build new and better things."

Shortly after his marriage, he went to Skyleaf Basin to maintain the irrigation system—that was the first time he saw a direct causal link between the pipes he repaired and the living people. The pipes were working → the waterwheels were turning → the flower fields came to life → the flowers became medicine → the patients recovered. Red was the most striking visual marker on that causal chain. Later, he dressed Anna in red, perhaps without explicitly thinking "because of the flowers of Skyleaf"—but the roots were there.

He died when Anna was 13. Not an accident, but a chronic illness—related to radiation exposure. When Anna was about 9, Edmund, as a guardian mechanic, transported a radioactive Axiom stray component to a settlement (the component was a civilization-level contribution to the local area). Edmund was unaware of the radiation risk during the transport—he only learned of the exposure afterward. He chose not to tell anyone (or very few). He didn't want the shadow of death to taint the time he spent with Anna. The Red Jacket incident (when Anna was 10) and his subsequent actions of taking Anna out and traversing the settlements to create his own web all occurred after he knew his time was running out. Anna went out with him a few times, but Edmund visited far more settlements than Anna was present at. Anna had time to prepare, but she didn't know the true cause of her illness—until Episode 61 aligned the radiation timeline with the Red Jacket creation timeline. This conclusion wasn't explicitly stated by any character; it belonged solely to Anna.

### Miriam Freedman

| Parameter | Value |
|------|------|
| Gender | Female |
| Identity | Anna's mother, logistics coordinator, currently residing in Eagle's Nest |

**[Personality – Let Actions Speak for Themselves]**

Miriam wasn't a perceiver like Edmund. She was the kind of person who knew everyone in Atheria by name, whose child was sick, and which airships had scheduling conflicts next month. Her ability wasn't to hear voices—she could read people.

The first rule she taught Anna when they were picking sunberries was, "Pick only the ripest ones, leave two-thirds for the birds and the seeds." This was the first rule about sustainability Anna learned. She also taught Anna to observe people—after returning from market day, she would ask at the dinner table, "What direction was that old lady's son looking when she was selling pottery?" Anna thought for a moment: "He was looking at the stall next door." Miriam nodded: "Because he wants to learn blacksmithing, but his mother wants him to inherit the pottery business. You noticed that."

After Edmund's death, Miriam did not break. She continued to function in a way that Anna did not understand at the time but later came to understand—not "strong," but "living in a different shape but still alive."

### Lia Redwood（莉亚）we only call lia and their parent first name in novel

| Parameter | Value |
|------|------|
| Gender | Female |
| Age at the start of the story | 13 years old |
Height/Weight | 148cm / 38kg (Slower development due to chronic lung problems) |
| Body type | Slender and thin, with narrow shoulders and long, thin limbs |
| Skin tone | Warm white with a hint of honey |
| Hair | Reddish-brown (auburn with copper undertones), naturally wavy, medium length |
| Eyes | Grayish-green with a brown undertone, shifting between gray and green depending on the light. |
| Face | Soft contours, round chin, hasn't completely lost the roundness of childhood |
| Jewelry | Two small gold-colored earrings, made of brass |
| Signature Outfit | Loose-fitting linen/cotton long-sleeved shirt + overalls or loose-fitting trousers + soft-soled shoes |
| Physical Characteristics | Walking pace is slightly slower than peers; requires adjustment of breathing after strenuous activity; occasional slight panting |
Parents | Kaelan and Sara (Redwood), Atheria residents |

**[Physical Condition - Gray Lung]**

Lia's lung problem is not an individual genetic defect—it's a phenotype of the Sky Fire genetic imprint carried by the entire Archeon population (see §3.3b). The Atheria call it "gray lung." One or both of Lia's parents carry the defective allele but do not express it. Lia has encountered an unfavorable combination.

Clinical manifestations: Alveolar gas exchange efficiency is about 15-20% lower than normal. The patient can manage daily life independently. Climbing hills, stairs, or any activity that raises the heart rate above a certain threshold will cause significant shortness of breath. Dust and hot steam environments worsen symptoms. Recovery from acute injuries (such as rib fractures leading to pulmonary contusion) is slower and more complex than in healthy individuals.

Current Treatment Status: Ember Crown extract is currently the most effective known symptom relief method—it cannot cure the disease, but it can improve alveolar function. Long-term use can increase gas exchange efficiency to near-normal levels (still 5-8% lower, but with significant functional improvement). Ember Crown grows only in the Skyleaf Basin.

[In Episode 35, the term "grey lung" is unnecessary in Sara's context with Anna—Sara says, "She has lung problems; she's had them since she was little."]
[Lia herself said, "Mom said it was something from when I was little"—this statement still holds true because the version Sara/Kaelan gave her was vague.]
[The term "grey lung" exists only at the world-building level; it is used in character dialogues in natural language.]

**[Personality – Let Actions Speak for Themselves]**

Lia is the kind of kid who sits in the corner of the workshop watching you fix something, silently observing for two hours, and then says, "You installed that washer backwards." Her dry humor is a form of self-protection—using accurate observation instead of emotional expression.

She walks slowly not because she's lazy—it's because her body won't allow it to be fast. A lung condition makes her unsuitable for prolonged exposure to dust and hot steam. Atheria's apprenticeship is "learning by hand"—entering a workshop at 13, mentored by a master, hammering every day. Lia has no master. No workshop will take her. Not rejection—it's a practical judgment: her lungs can't handle it. In a world where "who you are" equals "what you can do," Lia's hands are empty.

**"Everything is ultimately a lever"** is not a clever saying. It's the answer a person forced to work empty-handed in a world where "skill equals status" spent 13 years observing from the sidelines: If I can't exert force directly, I'll find a fulcrum. It's not a byproduct of observation—it's the long-term reflection of someone excluded from participation on "how can I participate?"

She thus saw things that those who walked fast missed. This keen observation stemmed from a forced slowing of the pace—a sentiment that resonates implicitly with Anna's idea of ​​"stopping to listen." But observation comes at a price: she understood what others held, while possessing nothing of her own.

[Lia inner engine] Her underlying problem wasn't "Can I get better?"—it was "Do I have a place on the loom?"
She doesn't feel self-pity. She doesn't blame fate. But the problem remains beneath the surface.
It drove all subsequent actions: picking up the wrench (Ep34), lantern + lantern lingering friction (Ep37).
; Ep41 Flower Field "Did the soil give color to the flowers, or did the flowers give color to the soil?" (Connecting directions)
"Help me check the ratchet" (Ep63), Ep64 friction beat (blocked by the body → flip shell solution),
Bolts (Ep65), independent repair latches (Ep67). Each step answers the same question.
; 
Friction beat distribution:
; Ep37 Lantern Delay = First Friction (Seeing peers' hands move quickly → Seeing one's own hands → Gone) = Passive Contrast
Ep64 drive chain = Second friction (tried → stopped → given a solution → went) = Active steering
The two friction clamped down on all the positive progression beats in the middle (wrench → valve check → winch → bolt).
The goal is to prevent progress from being a "straight upward" process—to achieve precision growth after "knowing where the boundaries are."

### Milo

| Parameter | Value |
|------|------|
| Gender | Male |
| Age at the start of the story | 14 years old |
Height/Weight | 147cm / 43kg |
| Body type | Slender, not fully developed, with thin limbs but nimble fingers |
| Skin tone | Dark brown |
| Hair | Dark brown/black curly hair, short and voluminous |
| Eyes | Dark brown, large and bright, curious and focused |
| Signature Outfit | Grayish-brown loose-fitting shirt + dark brown short jacket (oversized) + cargo pants + goggles |
| Occupation | Anna's apprentice, trainee airship mechanic |

**[Personality – Let Actions Speak for Themselves]**

Milo is the kind of apprentice who, when you turn around, has already handed you the tools you haven't even asked for. It's not about being ingratiating—he's observing your work rhythm and anticipating your next move. The questions he asks are more precise than one would expect for someone his age.

The jacket is too big, like it was given to me by someone else—this detail itself tells a story.

**(Milo and Kassia do not appear in the childhood chapters. They are characters introduced after Arc 1.)**

### Milo as a Mirror: Anna's Changing Control Patterns

Milo's narrative function is more than just apprenticeship. He's a mirror—showing us when Anna is relaxed and when she's tense.

Anna's control mode has a specific trigger: **"If this step goes wrong, the consequences fall on someone else."** It's not triggered by every task. The brakes are only applied when the consequences of failure affect other people. In safe contexts (normal flight, outposting, everyday life), Anna is relaxed, even playful. Control mode is only activated in specific situations.

This means that Milo saw two people in Anna:
Anna, the "safe context," is a woman who jokes around, deliberately lets Milo make mistakes and then watches how he handles them, and kicks the toolbox with her toe instead of handing it to him. Anna is a mentor Milo knows and enjoys following.
Anna when Stakes increased: Suddenly changed. She became faster, talked less, wouldn't let Milo touch anything critical, and did everything herself.

We see this shift through Milo's perspective. Milo doesn't understand why Anna sometimes trusts him and sometimes suddenly retracts all autonomy. We didn't understand it at first either. After the Lia incident, we retroactively understand: Anna doesn't distrust Milo—she distrusts **her own judgment under pressure**. The three-second pause is a brake on herself, and "doing everything herself" is a second layer of insurance—if I don't let others do it, others won't get hurt because of my wrong judgment.

**Mirror beats distribution:**

| Beat | Location | What Anna did | What Milo saw | What we saw |
|------|------|------------|-----------|-----------|
| M-1a | Ep12-15 | Jokes on deck/Kicking the toolbox with toes | Fun-loving instructor | Baseline: Anna can relax |
| M-1b | Ep12-15 | "I'll do this" (blocks Milo's hand) | There are some things she won't let you touch | Baseline: Control mode is present |
| M-Cover | **Ep25** (Before and after the Founder Day Race) | When the airflow changes abruptly, Anna's hand directly covers Milo's hand to guide him to the correct action. | "...You can just say 'left.'" | Anna's hand is faster than her mind. The body calculates "what if he makes a mistake?" in 24/7. |
| M - Withdrawal | **Ep33-34** (After Frontier Arrival) | Milo is doing a risky task. Anna extends her hand three inches out → stops → withdraws. Milo doesn't notice. **Kassia is present and notices.** | (Didn't see) | Anna's body wants to take over, but something stops her. Kassia sees—providing behavioral evidence for Ep37's "Counting Debts" |
| M-3a | **Ep42** Skyleaf Daily Life | Playful Interaction (Kassia in the Background) | Normal Instructor | Safe Context = Relaxation |
| M-3b | **Ep44** Skyleaf → Mountain section in-flight | Wanted to take over → Stopped → Let Milo finish → **exhale** | She hesitated but let me do it | The cost of Let go is a breath |
| M-4 | **Ep56 Prequel** (Kassia-Milo Dialogue) | "Lighter than you think" | Milo defends Anna | Milo understands the technical aspects. He still doesn't understand why. |
| M-5a | **Ep68** Morning (formerly Ep65) | Milo turns off the lights: "Let's go." Anna follows. | My first time overriding her | Milo's agency and Anna's control interlock. |
| M-5b | **Ep68** (formerly Ep65) | Laughed so hard I slipped and fell on the grass | Never seen Anna like this before | A day where control mode was completely absent |

**Progression direction:** From complete control → takeover during the process → almost takeover but stopped → complete let go → overridden.

**M-cover beat detail (anchored to Ep25 Founder Day Race):**
Test flights/warm-ups before a race or return flights after a race. Milo and Anna each pilot a skiff. A sudden change in airflow. Milo is adjusting a system. Anna judges that if Milo is too slow or makes a mistake, the plane will flip. Anna's skiff moves closer, her hand directly covering Milo's hand—not pushing it away, but **covering it and guiding his hand to perform the correct action**. One second. Done. Released. More significant than "I'll do this"—not pre-emptive interception, but in-process takeover. Milo was already doing it; Anna simply covered him.

Milo: "...You could just say 'left'."
Anna: "Next time."

Our first pass: a strict but capable pilot. Retroactive reparse: Anna's threat assessment operates 24/7.

**M-Shrinking Hand Beat Detail (Anchored to Ep33-34 Frontier):**
Ground task. Upon arriving at Frontier City, Milo is performing a slightly risky maintenance task (system checks near the docking platform). Anna is standing nearby. The crucial step—Anna's hand **moved**. It extended three inches. Then it stopped. It withdrew. Milo, focused, didn't notice. **Kassia was present—she saw this movement from her background presence in Ep33-34.** She didn't comment. But this visual information, combined with her observations over the next three days (the way Anna looked at Lia, Anna's excessive softness next to Lia), forms the behavioral chain of evidence for the "counting debts" judgment in Ep37. Kassia didn't make this judgment out of thin air—she saw what Anna's hand did in front of Milo.

We see that Anna's body still wants to take over, but something stops her. Perhaps it's "he should learn." Perhaps it's Edmund's way of doing things in the memory trilogy—Edmund never takes over Anna's hands.

[M-Withdrawal + Kassia Observation Skill Development]
This beat does two things simultaneously: mirror progression + establishing Kassia's ability as an observer.
Kassia can see behavioral patterns that Anna herself cannot—this ability is present in Arc 4.
This is the underlying foundation of Kassia-Anna dynamics. (Ep37 "Counting Debts", Ep42 "Flower Field Judgment")
; Ep58 Flats' "The universe doesn't need witnesses" is all based on "Kassia's ability to see things more accurately than others".

**M-3b (exhale beat detail) (anchored to Ep44 Skyleaf → high-altitude section):**
After Skyleaf departed in-flight, the ascent continued into the mountainous section. Milo needed to make a fast decision. Anna tried to take over—but stopped—let Milo do it—Milo made the right decision—Anna **exhaled**. This exhale was the release of all the accumulated tension in a single, complete moment of trust. We now have three data points (M-1b interception → coverage takeover → withdrawal → exhale), with a clear progression.

**The weight of M-5a (Ep68 Lights Out):** Milo overrides Anna for the first time. "Don't touch that thing today"—the apprentice says no to the master. This works because of the relationship built up by all the previous mirror beats. Anna's reaction is to go along with it—this is the first time the control mode has been completely overridden by someone else, and Anna doesn't resist. Not because Milo is right. It's because the trust has reached this level.

**Playful Anna Writing Rules:**
Anna and Milo's daily interactions are warm and humorous. She's not strict 24/7.
- Playful only appears in safe contexts (normal flight, outpost, daily without stakes).
- Once the stakes increase (someone may get injured), the switch is instant.
The speed of this switch itself is a mirror signal—the reader will notice "she was just laughing".
- **Don't write "playful" as "unprofessional."** Anna was joking while her hands were doing what they were supposed to do. It's the kind of competence Edmund had, "teasing you while tightening the bolt to the correct torque."

### Kassia

| Parameter | Value |
|------|------|
| Gender | Female |
| Age at the start of the story | Approximately 20-22 years old |
Height/Weight | 172cm / 60kg |
| Body Type | Slender, with clean lines, pilot physique – quick reflexes and strong core strength |
Skin tone | Light to fair, doesn't sunbathe often |
| Hair | Black, medium-short length, coarse texture, requires little maintenance |
| Eyes | Dark (almost black), sharp gaze, with a scrutinizing quality |
| Signature Outfit | Dark brown scarf/shawl (loosely wrapped around the neck and shoulders) + cropped top + cargo pants + goggles |
| Occupation | Airship pilot, pilot of the high-performance short-range airship "The Razor" |

**[Personality – Let Actions Speak for Themselves]**

Kassia is the kind of person who walks into a room without looking at anyone, yet knows exactly where everyone is. Her way of flying the Razor is completely different from Anna's way of flying the Razor—Anna "listens to the wind and works with it," while Kassia "reads the wind and is faster than it." Both methods result in a surviving landing, but they represent entirely different philosophies of interacting with the world.

Her scrutiny wasn't hostility—it was a habit of constant assessment. She assessed the risks, the distance, the people. She rarely spoke first, but every word she uttered was carefully weighed.

[★ Flight Method / Precision / Nihilism: Different Branches of the Same Tree]
The core of Kassia's worldview: The universe won't catch you. Parents disappear, but the world keeps turning. The Federation remains silent for 500 years.
The psychological inference from this premise is: if the world won't catch you, you can only be faster than the world.
;
Anna's "listen to the wind and cooperate with it" approach is based on trust—the wind will give you information, and you and it are part of the same system.
Edmund's teaching of "put your hand on it, wait, and let it tell you what it is" follows the same logic—basic trust in the material world.
Kassia doesn't have this trust. The premise of "reading the wind and then being faster than it" is that the wind won't cooperate with you; it only obeys the laws of physics.
You either beat it to the wind, or you die. She wasn't talking to the wind—she was outrunning it.
;
Precision (0.3mm eccentricity is unacceptable) is not the craftsman's perfection—it is zero tolerance for uncertainty.
tolerance range = entrusting a portion of the results to probability = trusting that "the world works normally most of the time".
Kassia doesn't trust most of the time. The parents disappeared on a normal flight—a probabilistic event, unpredictable, without warning.
Therefore: push all variables within your control to the limit. Under-correction might lead to failure, while over-correction only wastes energy.
Precision is Kassia's armor, not her virtue.
;
For Anna, flying is a connection (arrival → delivery → exchange with people).
Flight is a disconnection for Kassia (leaving → altitude → one person).
Two modes of flight coexisting in the same sky = one of the best philosophical dialogues in the entire book. No dialogue needed.
;
Causal chain overview:
Parents disappear (the world doesn't accept people) → The universe doesn't care (CI etching plate cosmic isolation) → Distrust of the world leads to self-correction.
; ├→ Flight: Faster than the wind. Anticipation. Preemptive correction. Never let your guard down.
Accuracy: 0.3mm is unacceptable. The result is not left to probability.
Interpersonal relationships: Always evaluating. Don't speak first. Weigh your words carefully.
Solo flight: No destination. Above it all. Avoiding density.
They are all different branches of the same root—"If the universe doesn't accept you, you can never let go."
;
Anna/Lia's erosion path (through flight precision penetration):
; Ep38 slot — Kassia sees "listening to the wind" working for the first time (survival without control)
Ep57 — Testing "No Correction" → Not Dead → Data Point Archive
Ep60 — Precision protected Anna → Skill changed from armor to care
Ep62 — correction angle reduced → waited one more beat → trust +0.01
Ep69 — Hand on the wall. Not reading anything, not predicting anything. Doing nothing. Just sitting there.
Kassia spent his entire life using his hands to control things—joysticks, calibration rings, winches. His hands were tools. His hands were armor.
The hand touching the wall did nothing. This is the Kassia version of "put your hand on it and wait."
She didn't know. But her hands did.

**(Kassia does not appear in the childhood chapters. She is a key character after Arc 1 and has significant interior development in Arc 4.)**

# Anna's Childhood Resource Library — Underlying Module v2

**Engine Definition:** What Anna noticed → Discovered an invisible chain → The world seemed to expand.
>
**Edmund's role:** Not a teacher, but someone who **shares joy**. His most frequent action is to wait by her side—for her to discover it herself—and then the two of them rejoice together. His response isn't a mechanical confirmation, but an entry point to shared delight—sometimes followed by a question ("So why do you think so?"), sometimes a pat on the back of her head, sometimes a memory that's been evoked in him, sometimes a shared smile. **Each response takes a different form—see Edmund's mandatory rules of dialogue variety.** Anna's childhood experience of "the world getting bigger" wasn't just cognitive—it was emotional. Every discovery was a joyful moment shared with Edmund.
>
**Miriam's role:** To teach Anna to see people, not things. Her perception is centered on social relationships, not physical systems.
>
**Red Line Rule:**
> - Childhood Red = Iron oxide red dye. The most common, cheapest, and ubiquitous.
> - All the botanical red is reserved for Skyleaf. Flower fields and Ember Crowns were absent from my childhood.
> - The appearance of red in childhood must be so casual that you wouldn't notice it on your first reading.
Edmund's explanation of the garment is that it is **the load-bearing point of the entire red line**—the surface is completely practical, and the deep layer is devastating.
Our first reaction upon reading it should be, "Oh, that makes sense." Our reaction upon reflection should be, "Heartbreak."
>
**Absolute Prohibition:** There is no scenario where the core of the scene is "Anna fixed something." She **noticed** something.

---

### Relationship between the two material systems

Anna's childhood footage in Timelapse exists in two formats:

```
Format A — Prose Paragraph (§1-§5 Perceptual Channel Stages + 1.1-1.5 Scenes)
Coverage: A sensory development framework spanning the entire age range of 3-13 years
Features: Stage descriptions + writing constraints + emotional tone words
Granularity: Stage level ("What channels are opened up at this age group?")

Format B — M-module card (M-06 to M-28)
Coverage: Specific memory scenarios for children aged 6-13
Features: Deployable episode footage + downstream potential tagging
Granularity: Scenario level ("What happened that afternoon")
```

The two are not duplicates—they differ in zoom level.

1.1-1.5 (Kite, Sunberry, Lizard, Rainstorm) = Prose Scenes for Ages 3-7. They are equivalent to "Unnumbered M-01 to M-05", but because they were written earlier, they are in full prose form rather than card format.

The card format starting with M-06 is the result of later standardization. The two overlap in the 6-7 age range (1.5 Rainstorm = 7 years old, M-07 Echo = 7 years old), but this is not a conflict—1.5 is the prose version, and M-07 is the card version, representing different scenarios for the same age.

**Search path when writing an episode:**
- First, check §1-§5 to confirm the sensory channel openness and writing constraints for this age group.
- Then search for specific deployable scenario materials in the M-module.
- Refer to both, without prioritizing one over the other.

---

## Module Format Description

Each card contains:
- **Age** / **Type of Perception** / **World Radius**
- **Scene Seed**: A short description of what happened.
- **Edmund's Moment**: What did Edmund do in this scene? What was his expression? What happened between the two people? In short, only describe the behavior and body language, not psychological analysis. If Edmund was not present, write "absent". This field forces the model to read Edmund's emotional presence as an independent dimension, preventing it from being overwhelmed by the perception training framework.
- **Invisible Chain**: What connection did Anna discover? (This item is "None" for pure Joy modules)
- **The role of an adult**
- **Emotional Tone**
- **Red Line Status**: Whether and how red appears in this module.
**Downstream Potential:** What moment might trigger this memory in the main storyline when the character is 19 years old?

---

# I. The Pure Sensory World (approximately 3-6 years old)

World radius: Eagle's Nest ± 50 meters. Anna's universe is home, boardwalk, front platform, rock wall.
There's no need for an invisible chain. She's just **living**.

---

### 1.1 First Dialect: Not a machine, but the wind

Before the hum of the workshop became part of her world, Anna's first dialect was sensory. Her understanding of life was not built on the cold logic of blueprints, but on sun-baked rocks, in the unexpected sweetness of a rare berry, and in the inexplicable sense of security in her father's arms.

Most children in the Assaria Valley grew up at the bottom of the basin—with compacted dirt roads underfoot, the clamor of the commune square in their ears, and the mingled scents of smoke and grass from the valley floor. Anna Friedman's childhood was four hundred meters higher than theirs.

Friedman's workshop—which Edmund called "The Eyrie"—clings to the upper rock face of the outer edge of the Asseria Canyon, half workshop and half home, its walkways and suspension bridges stretching into the open sky. From the former platform, one can see almost the entire basin: the shimmering reflections of the reservoir, a row of tiny white feathers dissipating in the wind from the steam vents in the middle of the cliff, the blurry silhouettes of farms and villages in the distance, and airships gliding slowly from one berth to another along the supply line.

On a clear day—and there are plenty of clear days in Athelia—standing on the railing of the Eagle's Nest, the world appears layered. Below lies the Ishka River at the bottom of the canyon, a thin, dark green line, its banks lined with neat terraces and irrigation canals. Above that is the mid-level of the basin: workshops and houses embedded in the cliff face, their windows shimmering like shards of gold in the afternoon light; metal skybridges span the abyss between the cliffs, always bustling with activity, appearing from this height like ants on a thread. Further up are towers and observatories, their blades perpetually spinning. Above that is the sky—Akerne's unique sky, tinged with the warm orange-white of K-type stars, the base of the clouds tinged with honey. At dusk, the Betelgeuse cloud emerges from the western horizon—first as a pale green arc from the deep blue, then as crimson filaments slowly unfold, like a giant net of red and green spread across the heavens.

When Anna was five, she could tell the time by color. A honey-colored sky meant it was about afternoon tea time. A copper-colored sky meant her father was almost done working. A deep blue sky with a touch of green—that was her mother calling her back inside, because the night wind was picking up.

### 1.2 The Embrace of the Kite and the Wind

Long before Anna understood the physics of lift and drag, she understood the joy of flying.

Her most prized toy was "The Sail"—made for her by Edmund from lightweight timber and a piece of gossamer-thin silver fabric cut from a discarded solar sail. It wasn't a complex mechanical device; it was a kite, something purely elegant. Anna would spend entire afternoons standing on the edge of the high cliff of Eagle's Nest, her little hands gripping the control line, waiting.

The moment of launch always made her hold her breath—the line taut, the sail in her hand like a living thing trying to break free, then the updrafts of the canyon caught it, and the silver sail shot up like a bullet, becoming a shimmering dot against the vast blue background. She learned to feel the wind with her hands—not data, but a living dance partner. A subtle tug meant a hot air mass was rising; a gentle slackening warned of an impending pause; a sudden lateral pull indicated the canyon walls were changing the direction of the airflow.

She would laugh out loud—the laughter carried away by the wind and disappearing into the echoes of the canyon—watching the little sail tumble, dive, and rise again in the air.

Once—when she was about six years old—a fierce gust of wind snatched the sail's control line from her hand. The sail, trailing the end of the line, flew across the canyon, higher and higher, until it finally caught in the canopy of a pine tree on the opposite cliff, shimmering in the wind like a miniature flag.

Anna didn't cry. She stared at the tree for a full five minutes, then turned to Edmund and said, "Daddy, I need a sail that can fly back."

Edmund crouched down, examined her empty hands with the same scrutinizing look one would give to parts inspectors, then glanced at the sail on the opposite tree, and nodded gravely: "Hmm. The recovery system malfunctioned."

What kind of recycling device?

"Your hand. Before letting go, wrap the string around your hand twice."

Anna thought for a moment: "The device wasn't broken, I just forgot about it."

Edmund laughed and hoisted her onto his shoulder: "Come on, let's see if we can borrow a long hook from Joren to get your sail down from that wretched tree."

They descended the boardwalk, passing through a three-story residential area. Joren—the basin's veteran mechanic, a burly man with a beard that could be tucked into his collar—after hearing the situation, without a word, pulled a three-meter-long telescopic pole from his tool rack and said, "Follow me, I know a way to get to the height of that tree."

The three of them—an adult, an older man, and a little girl perched on his shoulders—traveled for nearly an hour through the canyon's boardwalk system. As they crossed a skybridge, a herd of spiky rams carrying goods bleated past below; looking down from above, Anna thought their horns resembled gears. They passed a shop smoking meat, and the air suddenly filled with the aroma of smoke and spices. Anna's stomach rumbled, and Joren pulled half a piece of dry rations from his pocket and handed it to her: "An engineer can't work on an empty stomach."

Finally, they reached the whistling pine. The sail hung from the highest branch, fluttering gently in the wind, its silver fabric like a tiny star in the setting sun. Joren reached for it three times but couldn't reach it; on his fourth attempt, he nearly slipped off the rock face, but Edmund grabbed his belt. The three men looked at each other in bewilderment.

Finally, Anna solved the problem. She had Edmund lift her up to the highest branch she could reach, and she climbed up like a lizard—much lighter than two adults, the branch could easily support her—then untied the sail bit by bit from the pine needles, held it in her mouth, and climbed down.

Joren looked at the six-year-old girl with pine needles all over her head and a silver kite in her mouth, and said to Edmund, "Your apprentice is better than you."

### 1.3 The Scent of Sunshine and Mother's Hands

Akin's food is hearty and practical—rough bread from the valley floor, horned goat cheese, dried cured meat, vegetables from hydroponic greenhouses—but Miriam makes sure Anna's world isn't lacking in sweetness.

High on the sunlit rock face near Eagle's Nest, there are several stubborn, genetically enhanced shrubs that produce a small fruit called "Sun-Berry" for only a few weeks each year—the size of a fingertip, deep orange, and incredibly sweet, as if the whole summer has been condensed into a single pea.

Picking sunberries with her mother is Anna's most precious annual ritual.

They set off at dawn—a secret between the two of them. Miriam would slip a clean handkerchief into Anna's small pocket in her work clothes ("to wipe your hands, not your mouth—though I know you'll definitely use it"), and then the two of them would climb the rock face along a narrow path known only to them. The path was steep, requiring the use of both hands and feet in some places. Miriam would go up first in the difficult sections and then reach out to pull Anna up.

The shrubs grew in the crevices of the rocks, their roots incredibly deep—living things that took root in the impossible. Miriam taught Anna to pick only the ripest, darkest ones: "The lighter ones are still growing, leave them for next week. Pick only a third of each shrub, the rest for the birds and the seeds." This was the first rule about "sustainability" Anna learned, though she didn't know the word at the time.

Then came the eating. Sitting on the warm rocks, legs dangling over the canyon—a four-hundred-meter vertical drop unfolding beneath her feet, the Ishka River shimmering in the distance—Anna popped berries into her mouth one by one. The taste was pure sunshine bursting on her tongue, a sweetness so rare it was like a secret. She ate so fast that sticky orange juice dripped from her chin. Miriam gently wiped it away with her thumb, her laughter as warm as the sunshine.

"Eat slowly, little engineer. They're not going to fly away."

"But they're so delicious—"

That's why we need to slow down. Good things deserve to be savored.

Anna didn't understand the sentence at the time. Twenty-year-old Anna will suddenly understand at some unexpected moment—perhaps in a market in an unfamiliar settlement, when someone hands her a fruit of a similar color.
[Forward pointing] → Ep33 Thread Festival Market Sunberry Trigger (the closing position of this hook)
[middle echo] → Ep27 Story Night (Sunshineberry is externalized for the first time through narration)

One year, when Anna was about eight years old, she decided to bring some sunberries back for Edmund. She carefully wrapped a small handful in a handkerchief and protected them all the way. But when she ran back to the workshop, panting, and proudly opened the handkerchief, she found that the berries had turned into a puddle of orange mush from her body heat and the jostling.

Her face fell.

Edmund looked down at the puddle of paste, his expression extremely serious: "What is this? A new formula for valve grease?"

"It's a sunberry! I—I wanted to bring it for you to eat..."

"Let me try." He dipped his finger in the orange paste, tasted it, then closed his eyes, a look of exaggerated ecstasy on his face. "This is the best valve grease I've ever eaten."

Anna stopped crying and started laughing.

Miriam walked in and saw the father and daughter, each with a hand full of orange paste, exchanging silly grins. She sighed, "I shouldn't have let you two stay in the same room."

This is the taste of safety. It's not that there are no failures—it's that when you fail, someone laughs with you.

### 1.4 The Secret World on the Rock Wall: Lizards and Flowers

The rocky platform outside the Eagle's Nest is not a sterile plane; it is a miniature universe, and Anna is its patient observer.

Her closest companions were the slate lizards basking in the sun on the warm steel plates of the landing platform. They had swift, dart-like hunting movements, and also slow, meditative stillness—when basking, they would remain completely still, only the small scales below their throats rising and falling slightly with their breath. Anna named the largest one "Chip" because a small scale on its back was missing a corner. She would leave a little water in a bottle cap where Chip often appeared, then squat a meter away, watching it drink like a small stone statue.

The lizard taught her the art of quiet, patient observation—the presence of another life without needing to control it. This is different from mechanical logic. You can take a machine apart, understand it, and fix it; with a lizard, you can only wait for it to trust you. Anna instinctively understood this difference at the age of six.

One day, "Chip" lingered on the back of her hand for a full three seconds—the cool little claw, the slight weight, the rough texture of the scales. Anna held her breath, her heart pounding as if it would explode, until the lizard hopped away again. Only then did she exhale and run into the workshop, yelling at Edmund, "It stepped on me! It stepped on me! Chip stepped on my hand!"

Edmund didn't even look up: "Your hand passed its structural integrity test. Congratulations."

"Dad, what are you saying?"

"I'm saying Chip thinks your hands are strong enough to be its landing pad."

She was equally fascinated by the blue hyacinths that tenaciously grew in the cracks of the rocks. These flowers shouldn't have been there—on a wind-eroded rock face four hundred meters high, there was no soil to speak of, only a hair-thin crack and a tiny bit of moisture seeping from who-knows-where. But they were there, their petals trembling in the slightest breeze, their blue as thin as dyed morning mist. Anna would lie on the cool stone watching them for hours, tracing the veins of the petals with her fingers, pressing her cheek against the flowers to feel their incredible softness.

"Mom, why do these flowers grow in such a difficult place? It's much warmer in the basin below."

Miriam crouched down and looked at the cluster of flowers, then thought for a moment: "Maybe they don't find it difficult. Maybe for them, this is home."

"But the wind is so strong."

"So their roots go very deep. Look—" Miriam pointed to the roots in the crack, three times thicker than the flower stems—"They look small and soft on top, but are stronger than steel cables underneath."

Anna thought for a long time. Then she said, "Like something Daddy made."

Miriam smiled. "Yes. Probably."

This isn't an analysis of "bioengineering." It's the simple, profound wonder of a child discovering the texture of the world and life. But later, Anna did learn a rule from the hyacinth—not taught, but a conclusion she drew for herself: **Things that survive in the most unlikely places often have roots you can't see.**

### 1.5 The smell of rain and the absolute safety in my father's arms

Rain is a rare, magical event in the Athelia Valley. It may come twice a year, heralded by the sharp, ozone-filled smell and the deep, warm, damp dust—the life-giving, thirst-quenching scent rising from the basin plains.

The memory that made her feel safe forever happened during a sudden downpour. She was about seven years old.

That afternoon, she and Edmund were inspecting a section of railing on the boardwalk outside the Eagle's Nest—more of a stroll than work, Edmund tapping on bolts and shaking support bars as they walked, Anna following behind and mimicking him. Within ten minutes, the clouds on the horizon changed from honey to lead gray, then to a deep purple Anna had never seen before.

"Dad, that color—"

"run."

They hadn't run three steps when the sky split open.

It wasn't the kind of rain that drizzles. It was the kind of rain that pours down like a wall of water from the sky. The downpour in Aselia came and went quickly, but for those ten or so minutes, the world dissolved into the roar of water. Visibility dropped to less than a meter. The rainwater on the boardwalk immediately went up to our ankles.

Before fear could take root, Edmund had already scooped her up and stuffed her into his heavy, worn leather jacket.

That memory was a symphony of senses: the sting of cold rain on her face, the resounding drumbeats of rain pounding on leather—just inches from her ear—and then, overwhelming everything, the feeling of being completely enveloped in her father's strength. She was surrounded by that scent—the scent that signified "home": the spiciness of engine oil, the richness of old leather, and a warmth unique to Edmund. His heartbeat was in her ear, steady as the perpetual bass note beneath a river stone.

At that moment, being held in his arms, the raging storm outside was just a song.

He was still walking—steadily making his way back to the workshop along the boardwalk, his pace neither quickening nor frantic, one hand firmly supporting her. Anna peeked out from the gap in her leather jacket; the rain was like a flowing silver screen, the distant canyon walls appearing and disappearing in the mist.

"Dad, aren't you afraid?"

"What's there to be afraid of? The rain isn't here to arrest anyone. It's just busy with its own things."

When they reached the workshop entrance, he put her down. Both of them were soaked from head to toe—to be precise, Anna was half-soaked (her leather jacket covered most of it), and Edmund was one hundred and twenty percent wet. His hair was plastered to his face, making him look like a large dog pulled from the river.

Anna laughed so hard at his disheveled appearance that she couldn't stand up from her squatting position.

Miriam opened the door and first saw a puddle of water, then two people laughing hysterically in the water. She took a deep breath: "...I'll go get the mop."

This is the feeling of absolute, unconditional security. It's not because there's no storm, but because there's someone in the storm who makes you feel—it's just rain, it's no big deal.


# ANNA FREEDMAN Growth Framework — Perceptual Channel Evolution Diagram v1.3

## Anti-gravity well protocol: From childhood to adulthood

This document aims to address the following issues:
>
The old model's failure mode—whenever Edmund + Anna scenes come up, the narrative gets sucked into a gravitational well of "mechanical diagnosis." As a result, childhood becomes a disordered training archive, with the core of each scene being "Anna hears an anomaly in a certain machine part." Wind turbine blade frequency deviation, water pump bearing wear, valve resonance misalignment... these things recur, compressing Anna's entire growth into a single line: **mechanic training timeline**.
>
The truth is: Anna's childhood was centered on **sensory, physical, and laughter-filled**. Mechanics were merely one of many languages ​​Edmund spoke (and not his native language), not the engine of the scenes.
>
**Core Principle:** Only specific perceptual channels are opened for each age group. This diagram strictly controls when and how a machine is allowed to enter the narrative focus.

---

## §0 Anti-gravity well rule (essential reading before writing any Anna scenario)

### Gravity Well Diagnosis

**Gravity Well #1: Mechanical Diagnostic Well**

If any of the following scenarios appear in your writing, you've fallen into the mechanical diagnostics well:

- The core event of the scenario is "Anna diagnoses a mechanical malfunction".
Anna's descriptions of perception focus on technical parameters such as frequency, deviation, and vibration modes.
- A passage that should have been an emotional scene reads like a repair manual.
Wind towers, water pumps, valves, bearings, pressure systems—the density of these words surpasses the density of human behavior and physical sensations within the scene.
Anna's "listening with her eyes closed" resulted in a diagnostic report rather than a bodily sensation.
- The only objects of perception are infrastructure (pumps, wind towers, solar panels, waterwheels) – forgetting that Archeon is a world with curved stone arch bridges, twin towers, grasslands, canyons, glaciers, and airship routes.

**Gravity Well #2: Edmund Intermediate Well**

If your scenario falls under any of the following categories, you've fallen into Edmund's mediator well:

- Every time Anna made a sensory discovery, Edmund had to "wait nearby" for it to be completed.
Anna never perceives things on her own—she is always guided.
Edmund's dialogue mainly explains how a technological system works.
We feel that Anna's childhood was an "Edmund-mediated perception archive"—every discovery was relayed through Edmund.
More than half of the M-module structures follow the pattern: "Edmund et al. → Anna discovers → the two are happy."

**Correct Proportion:** Edmund's perceived experiences ≈ 50%. The other 50% is Anna's **own** experience—she's alone, crouching by the rock face watching lizards; she's alone, standing on the stone arch bridge listening to the wind whistling through the arches; she's alone, on the boardwalk at dusk, noticing the sky's color is different from yesterday. We are in her mind, seeing with her eyes, hearing with her ears. Edmund is not in the picture. **These independent perceptual scenes are the true engine of "growing up with Anna from her perspective."**

### The Ending Point of Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind × Violet Evergarden

Anna's perception allows her to "hear" the health of the physical system—this is the Valley of the Wind. But the health of the physical system directly reflects the living conditions of people—this is the Violet Evergarden.

A settlement's sound chords would be incomplete without the periodic creaking of the winch of the hoisting cage—not just "one cage has stopped," but "the mid-level workshop on the cliff may have lost freight today, and materials for some people won't arrive." Standing on a stone arch bridge, she heard the sound of water beneath it was louder than the last time she was there—not just "the water level has risen," but "the upstream snowmelt is earlier than usual, and this year's planting time may need to be adjusted."

Every unusual thing Anna heard was a story about a person.

This is why her perception cannot be compressed into "listening to the pump casing." The pump casing is merely the smallest, most immediate object of perception. Her field of perception is the entire landscape—the curving arches, the grasslands beyond the twin towers, the shifting air currents along the airship's path, the echoing shapes of the canyons, the cracks of the glaciers. These enormous objects of perception and a water pump are different scales of the same capability. And anomalies at each scale ultimately point to humanity.

### How to escape

Ask yourself three questions:

1. **Which age-specific sensory channel is Anna using in this scenario?** (Refer to the channel table in §1-§5 below) — If she is 6 years old, she cannot be listening to the bearings. If she is 19 years old, the mechanic diagnosis may appear, but not the scene engine.
2. **If you remove all the technical terms, what's left of the scene?** —If the scene is empty after removing them, you're writing a manual, not a novel. The correct state: After removing the technical terms, the scene still has someone doing something, experiencing physical sensations, and engaging in emotional actions.
3. **What is the last image in this scene?** — If it's "she determines the location of the malfunction" → Gravity Well. If it's a person's expression, a body movement, or silence → Safety.

### Absolute Prohibition

The core of no scene is "Anna fixed something." She notices the thing. She interacts with the thing. In the process of fixing it, she touches upon relationships and memories. The fixing itself is never the climax.


**Anna can perceive large-scale objects (more "Valley of the Wind" than any pump):**

- The curved stone arch bridge—the wind blows through from beneath the arch, distorting the sound; standing on the bridge, she feels the vibrations at a different frequency under her feet than at either end of the bridge.
- Twin-tower structure - A wind tunnel effect is created between the two towers; when she went in, her hair was sucked to one side, and when she came out, the wind direction suddenly changed.
- The grassland unfolding behind the tower—a visual scale shock, a sudden transition from a vertical canyon world to a horizontal one, sound changing from "bounced between walls" to "scattering in all directions and then disappearing."
The airship was in the clouds—visibility was zero. Anna relied entirely on her sense of touch and hearing to fly, using changes in air temperature to indicate the thickness of the clouds.
- The echo shape of the canyon—she shouted into the canyon, and the time difference and superposition of the echoes told her how wide the canyon was at that location and what material the opposite cliff was made of.
- The low-frequency crack of glaciers—the sound of the earth, that deep bass that comes from afar, rising from the soles of your feet to your knees.
- River bends—the sound of water scouring the outer bend and the silence of sedimentation on the inner bend, two sounds coexisting ten meters apart.
- The changing light at dusk—the long twilight of Akin's 27-hour day, with colors ranging from honey to copper to deep blue-green, each stage accompanied by different temperatures and scents.

These scenes don't require Edmund's presence. Anna walks alone across a stone arch bridge, the wind whistling through the arch, and she pauses for a second—we feel that within her. This is what "growing up with Anna" means. It's not about watching someone else teach her. It's about **being herself**.

## M-06 · River Stone (6-8 years old)

**Deployment Location:** Ep23 Sensory Trigger. Anna squats down by the river to wash her hands → Water Temperature Trigger.
**Function:** The first link in the entire book's chain of perception and inheritance. The seed of "the relationship between things".
**Rule:** Happiness is the engine; listening is a byproduct. This is not a training scenario. It's about shared delight.

---

The river water was cold. Anna pulled her hand back as she put it in—it was June, but the meltwater from the snow-capped mountains still carried the temperature of winter.

Edmund was already lying on the rock.
