The first time she went, she stood outside for a long time. She didn't know what to bring. She had nothing in her hands. What should a mechanic bring when visiting someone she'd hurt? An apology? Food? Flowers? Atheria doesn't have these customs. Not that they don't—it's just that Anna didn't know what to bring. In the end, she brought nothing. She went in empty-handed.

Lia was in bed. Smaller than Anna remembered. The seven-year-old looked only five, huddled under the blanket. Her eyes were on Anna. There was no fear. No welcome. Just looking.

Anna didn't know what to say.

She stood by the bed. Lia looked at her. Anna looked at Lia. There was a causal chain between the two of them—from the workshop to the walkway, from the wrench to the cast iron coupling, from Anna's hand to Lia's left rib—this line stretched between them, invisible but known to both of them.

Anna sat down. On the stool beside the bed. She didn't say "I'm sorry"—not because she didn't want to, but because those two words weren't enough. And she couldn't explain why they weren't enough.

Lia spoke first.

"Your jacket is very red."

Anna glanced down at herself. The red jacket. She'd forgotten she was still wearing it.

"Um."

"My mom's scarf is also red, but not as red as yours."

This is a seven-year-old doing what a seven-year-old does—comparing what's in front of her with what she knows. Lia isn't talking about what happened that day. She's talking about colors.

Anna sat for a while. Not long. She didn't know how long she should stay. Lia started coughing—a congenital cough, unrelated to her injury—Anna stood up, wanting to do something, but not knowing what to do, and finally she retreated to the doorway. There was an adult at the door—probably the person looking after Lia—giving her a look that said, "You can leave now."

Anna is gone.

She went a few more times after that. Each time she went empty-handed. Each time she didn't know what to say. Each time Lia would say something unrelated to that day—about the bugs she saw in the room, about the soup being too hot, about the leaves on the tree outside the window turning yellow. Anna listened. Occasionally she would respond with a word.

Every time she left, she felt she should have done more. But she didn't know what "more" meant.

In winter, Lia could go out. Short distances. She still walked slowly—slower than before. Anna sometimes saw her from afar on the boardwalk. A red jacket and a slowly walking child were separated by the entire length of the boardwalk. Anna didn't go over.

She began to maintain a distance. Not intentionally—or perhaps intentionally, but she wouldn't admit it. Close contact had hurt a child. Her body had welded "closeness" and "danger" together. A three-second pause controlled her hands. But not her feet—her feet began to automatically choose the farther path.

This distance will only grow further and further over the next six years.

### Anna-Lia · Six-year interval (14-19 years old baseline)

After Anna flew away at the age of 14, she returned to Atheria every three to four days. Lia lived at the bottom of the valley. Anna lived in the eagle's nest on the upper cliff. The physical distance was not short—from the eagle's nest to the bottom of the valley, one had to take a lift and traverse different passageways. Anna's route back was: airport → eagle's nest → valley maintenance point → home. She didn't need to pass through the area where Lia lived.

However, it's unrealistic to expect complete zero interaction within Atheria's 30,000-person community.

A more accurate baseline:

```
Anna flew away when Anna was 14 (Lia was 8). On her return, she occasionally saw Lia in the distance on the boardwalk.
A slow-moving child is at the bottom of a valley. He doesn't actively try to reach it.

15-16 years old (Lia 9-10 years old): Nodding in the crowd at a festival gathering. At the same stall on market day.
We brushed past each other. The distance between us—knowing each other but having no reason to speak.

At age 17 (Lia was 11), Miriam once mentioned, "Lia is doing better lately."
Anna didn't press further. Miriam didn't say much either.
There was a silent consensus between the two women: not to delve into the topic.

18-year-old Anna (Lia was 12) returned to Atheria to perform maintenance and repairs at the cliffside workshop.
While having lunch at the Common House at the bottom of the valley, Lia sat at the other end of the long table.
There were five or six people between them. Anna saw Lia coughing—
That short, controlled cough. The cough of gravidarum.
Anna's finger paused briefly on the rim of the bowl. Then she continued eating.
They did not speak.

19 years old (Lia 13 years old), Arc 2 begins. Baseline temperature:
We lived in the same canyon for six years, knew each other but never really talked.
Anna knew Lia's name, knew she walked slowly, and knew she was still alive.
Lia knew Anna was the pilot in the red jacket.
There is a causal connection between the two that has never been spoken of.
```

The temperature was just right—cool enough—there was tension when they first appeared named in Arc 2. Yet warm enough—there was no hostility between them. Only Anna's one-sided guilt and distance.

[Six years on Lia's side]
Lia has grown up in these six years. From age 7 to 13. Her ribs have long since healed.
; polio is chronic—it doesn't get better or suddenly worsen (until it gets worse).
Her life at the bottom of the Atheria Valley consisted of: walking slowly, observing much, and being rejected by workshops.
They developed a mindset that "everything is ultimately a lever."
Her attitude towards Anna wasn't resentment—it was closer to a watchful neutrality.
She knew the man in the red jacket. She knew he came back every three or four weeks.
She didn't go over to her. Anna didn't come over.
This symmetrical distance is the result of the choices made by each of the two individuals.
Anna chose guilt-driven avoidance, but what did Lia choose? (Open question)
(Resolved between Ep65-67: Lia knows. Always knew. Distance is an informed choice.)

CRITICAL [## §1 The Pure Sensory World (Ages 3-6)]

**World Radius:** Eagle's Nest ± 50 meters. Universe = Home, Boardwalk, Front Platform, Rock Wall.

**Open Channel:**

**How ​​machines exist at this stage:**

The sounds from the workshop were background noise—evidence that "Dad's here." Anna didn't distinguish between the lathe and the forging hammer; she differentiated between "Dad doing quiet work" and "Dad doing noisy work." She smelled the machine oil because it smelled like her father, not because she was learning about lubrication systems.

If Edmund says something about machines at this stage, its function is:

What he said | What we thought | Actual function |
|--------|-----------|---------|
"Your hand passed its structural integrity test" | Mechanic Dad's Technical Humor | Turning the incident of the lizard stepping on her into a joke between father and daughter |
| "The recycling device malfunctioned" (referring to her letting go of the kite and it flying away) | Technological metaphor | A language game between two people—Edmund teases her with the language of mechanics |
"This is the best-tasting valve grease I've ever eaten." | Comfort | Turn failure into laughter |

**Commonality:** Every instance of "technical jargon" was designed to make Anna laugh, not to teach her.

**Scenarios where Edmund is not present (≥50% of content for ages 3-6):**

Anna didn't only perceive the world when she was next to Edmund. Much of her sensory experience occurred independently:

She sat alone on the platform watching the lizard—for the entire afternoon, without any adults around. The water in the bottle cap, the meter-long distance, the silence like a small stone statue. **No one saw** when Chip stepped on her hand. She held her breath, waited for the lizard to jump away, and **then** ran inside to call Edmund. This "then" is important—the perception occurred in solitude, the sharing followed.
She lay alone on the rock face, looking at the hyacinths—"Mommy, why do these flowers grow in such difficult places?" She asked this question only after observing them for several hours. Observation comes first, adulthood second.
She stood alone by the Eagle's Nest railing, learning to guess the time from the color of the sky—no one taught her "honey color = afternoon tea." She discovered it herself. Because she stood there watching every day.
She and her mother are picking sunberries—not a scene from Edmund. This is Miriam's axis: taste, touch, sustainability rules, and the intimacy between mother and daughter.

In these scenes, we live directly inside Anna's body. There's no Edmund's mechanics or humor to buffer it. It's a direct interaction between a little girl and the world.

**Emotional keywords for this stage:** Safety, curiosity, laughter, sweetness, warmth, and excitement.

---

## §2 Body and Rhythm (6-8 years old)

**World Radius:** Eagle's Nest → Basin. Begin descending—boardwalk, sky bridge, riverside.

**Newly opened channel:**

| Channel | Content | Representative Scenarios |
|------|------|---------|
| Touch → Hearing Bridging | Lying on a River Stone to "Listen" to the Flowing Water – Hands and Ears Working Simultaneously | M-06 River Stone |
| Rhythm Perception | The steady beat of the waterwheel, the intervals between hammer blows, the pitch changes as the wind blows through gaps of varying widths | Everyday Life |
| Social Observation (via Miriam) | "What direction was her son looking in when that old lady was selling pottery?" | Miriam Line |
| Spatial Hierarchy | The world is layered—river → terraced fields → cliffs → sky bridge → wind tower → sky | M-05 Layering |

**Still closed channels:**

- ❌ Mechanical diagnostics – Anna doesn't yet connect "listening" with "diagnosing faults".
- ❌ System-level understanding—She knew the waterwheel was turning, but didn't know it was connected to the irrigation system, the greenhouse, and the grain.

**The M-06 river stone was a key turning point—but it wasn't just mechanical training:**

The two lay on the same rock, foreheads almost touching. Anna heard water flowing beneath the rock. Edmund didn't say, "What frequency did you hear?" He himself listened for a while, his face pressed against the rock, and when Anna looked up, she saw him smiling—a smile of **joy**. The kind of joy a mechanic feels when they discover their child has seen what they've seen. Edmund's responses varied in every moment (see Edmund's rule of dialogue variety), but underlying it was the same shared delight.

The function of M-06 is not "Anna learned listening." It's "Anna and Edmund shared the joy of discovery." Listening is a byproduct; joy is the engine.

What Edmund might teach after M-06 (background exists, not the focus of the scene):
- Take her to see the waterwheel → but the key point is "Look at the water droplets on its blades glistening in the sunlight," not "Can you hear the blades rotating at a low speed?"
- Let her touch the temperature difference of a copper pipe → but the key is "This end is warm and that end is cool, guess why?" This kind of question, driven by natural curiosity, is not diagnostic training.

**Miriam's axis unfolds in parallel during this phase:**

Miriam didn't teach "listening to things"—he taught "reading people." The conversations at the dinner table after market day were **social observation training**, forming a dual-track system with Edmund's physical perception training. Anna's future full capabilities came from the convergence of these two lines—at 19, she could simultaneously "hear" the problems of a pump and "see" the social tensions of a settlement because she had two teachers, not just one.

**Emotional keywords for this stage:** Discovery, rhythm, resonance, sharing, curiosity has found companions.

---

## §3 The World Opens Up (Ages 8-9)

**World Radius:** Basin → Flying Out. First long-haul flight (family trip). The world changes from "my home" to "there are distant places."

**Newly opened channel:**

| Channel | Content | Representative Scenarios |
|------|------|---------|
| Aerial Sensations | The feeling of being pushed back during takeoff, deck vibration, and changes in airflow are transmitted through the body | M-09 Flight Segment |
| Macro Vision | Atheria Shrinks from the Air—A Reversed Version of the Layered World in M-05 | M-09 |
| Soundscape Integration | "The Temperament of a Cliff"—Not a Single Frequency, but the Sounds of an Entire Place | M-14 Chords |
| Environmental Forecast (via Miriam) | "Birds are arguing. It's going to rain." | M-08 Birdsong |

**Key Turning Point—M-14 Chord:**

The first sound Anna heard wasn't the sound of "a part"—it was the sound of "a cliff face." This was geological listening, hearing on a geological/climatic scale, not a mechanical one. She heard the wind whistling through layers of rock of varying densities, water flowing through cracks, the low frequencies of a distant waterfall, birds changing their calls—all of these blended into a **chord**.

Edmund waited nearby, waiting for her to say it herself. When she did, Edmund's reaction wasn't "correct"—it was **shared delight**.

**M-14's function: To expand Anna's listening from a "single object" to the "environment as a whole." However, the expansion focuses on nature/geology, not mechanics.**

**What is flight at this stage:**

This wasn't a skills demonstration. It was a typical family outing—a family going to the market in an airship. Edmund's hand on Anna's shoulder wasn't because he was worried she'd fall; he was excited too. Miriam was also on the airship. Flight = family unity = an infinitely vast world.

**Still closed channels:**

- ❌ Mechanical Diagnosis – Anna might notice the sound of the airship engine, but her reaction would be "this sound is warm" or "it's humming," not "the RPM is 200 rpm too low."
- ❌ Independent repairs—she observes Edmund's work, but not from an apprentice's perspective.

**The Real Engine of M-09 Thread Festival:**

"Someone wants the wind to sing"—Anna heard an organ but didn't know what it was. Edmund forgot to eat his grilled yellow skewers because he was listening too. At the closing ceremony, riding on someone's shoulders to see the lights—hands gripping ankles tightly.

The core visual element isn't technology. It's the lighting, the music, the festive atmosphere, the family of three, and the person riding on someone's shoulders.

**The world opens up = a leap in the scale of perceived objects:**

At age 8-9, Anna left the vertical world of the canyon for the first time. The sound changed from "bounced between the walls" to "spreading in all directions." This scale shock itself was a massive perceptual event—requiring no mechanical presence:

The instant she flew out of the canyon—the cliffs on both sides suddenly disappeared, the sky transformed from a slit into a full dome, and Anna's ears suddenly went "empty" because there was no echo.
- The first time I saw a landscape completely different from Atheria from the air - perhaps hills, undulating green waves stretching all the way to the horizon, the complete opposite of the vertical tiers of the canyons.
- I landed in a place that wasn't home—the soil beneath my feet was a different color, a different hardness, the humidity in the air was different, even the wind smelled different.

These were Anna's own body shocks. It wasn't Edmund pointing and saying, "Look over there." It was her body suddenly entering a new physical space, all her senses needing to recalibrate simultaneously. We experienced this recalibration with her—that's what "growing up together" means.

**Emotional keywords for this stage:** Opening up, flying, distant places, overflowing, festivals, family.

---

## §4 ★ Doors through which machines are allowed (9-10 years old)

This is the most crucial paragraph in the entire framework. The old model made the mistake of opening this door too early, at age three, and doing so in the wrong way.

### The correct way to open a door

**M-13 Airship Breathing (9 years old): "It's breathing."**

Anna treated the machine as a living thing for the first time.

It wasn't Edmund who taught her—Edmund didn't say, "Come on, listen to the engine's breathing." Anna herself extended her already mastered listening skills (listening to water, rocks, and the temperament of cliffs) to something that could fly. She lay prone on some part of the airship (perhaps the keel beneath the airbags, or perhaps the outer wall of the engine compartment), listening as if she were lying on a river rock, and then spoke:

"It's breathing."

This door opens naturally, not through instruction.

Anna's path:
```
Listen to the sound of water (6 years old, M-06)
→ Listen to the cliff's temperament (9 years old, M-14)
→ Listen to the airship's breathing (9 years old, M-13)
```

Each step is a natural extension of the previous one. From liquid to solid to machine—but in Anna's perception, they are different surfaces of the same thing. She didn't "learn mechanical diagnosis"—she listened to a machine like she would listen to a stone, and discovered that machines also have tempers.

### Rules after the door is opened

Anna perceived the machine as a "living being with a temper," not as a "system that needs to be diagnosed."

Anna, aged 9-10, might say:
- ✅ "It's not happy today" (the airship engine sounds different)
- ✅ "This sound is wrong—it sounds like someone has a cold" (the abnormality of the pump is metaphorically described using the body)
- ✅ "It's sighing" (vapor release)
- ❌ "The second bearing on the left is out of sync" — This is the language of **19-year-old Anna**, not a 9-year-old.
- ❌ "Pump casing resonant frequency deviation of 0.3Hz" — This is not Anna's language at any age.

### Ages 10-12: From "Living Things" to "Relationships"

**M-15 (age 10) First time leaving Atheria:** The world becomes a map. Flying transforms from "the daily routine of sitting" into "discoveries made by oneself."

**M-18 (11-12 years old) sees a connection from the air:** Anna sees something Edmund hasn't noticed in twenty years of flying—underground water pipes "leaking" themselves through differences in vegetation.

The key to M-18: What Anna saw wasn't "pipelines"—it was **differences in vegetation color**. Her visual perception (an upgraded version of the layered world of M-05) told her "the grass on this line is greener than the grass next to it," and then her brain connected this visual information to "there are water pipes underneath."

This is not mechanical diagnosis. This is ecological observation combined with systems reasoning.

Edmund's reaction: "I've been flying for so many years, I've never noticed this before." — Genuine's surprise. She overtook him in the sky.

### Key Limitations at This Stage

Even if the door is opened, the machine is just one voice in Anna's world, not the main melody.

| Attention allocation and proportion for Anna aged 10-12 |
|-------------------------|------|
| Aerial perception during flight (wind, light, terrain) | 40% |
| Shared discovery with Edmund | 25% |
| The perception of machines as "living beings with tempers" | 15% |
| Miriam's Social Observations | 10% |
| Pure Fun – Friends, Holidays, Running, Climbing | 10% |

**Machines should never account for more than 15-20% of the attention. If the machine receives more than 50% of the attention in your scenario, you've fallen into a gravity well.**

**Emotional keywords for this stage:** Independence, the surprise of discovery is two-way, the sky, transcendence, and relationships.
]

---

## §5 Inheritance and Loss (Ages 12-13)

The world's radius is shrinking. Edmund is sick. The world falls from the sky back to the ground, from the basin back to the workshop, and from the workshop back to the bedside.

### 5.0 The World Closes—Anna's Identity Transition Timeline

The old version of intro_arc skipped a crucial stage. Anna's state at the time of the Lia incident (age 13, summer) was not "an apprentice mechanic already doing inspections"—it was "someone who had just been forced to fall from the sky back to the workshop and was painfully taking over her father's work."

```
Ages 3-9: Perceptive. Listen to the wind, the water, and the temperament of the cliffs. Fly with Edmund.
Machinery is the background (≤15%), existing as a "temperamental living thing".
—The world is opening up.

Ages 9-12: The Bloom Perception Period. M-13 airship breathing, M-18 seeing connections in the air.
Flight = the main source of pleasure. The mechanical door opens, but it's not the main theme.
—The world's largest.

[P-10 Edmund Radiation Exposure Hidden Timeline]
When Anna was 9 years old, Edmund transported Axiom components (which had a radiation hazard) to a settlement.
Edmund was unaware of the radiation. He only learned of it later. When Anna was 9 or 10 years old, Edmund became aware of his situation.
Anna was 10 years old. The M-16 red jacket was made by Edmund after he found out. Afterwards, Edmund traveled throughout the settlement weaving network, sometimes taking Anna with him.
Anna only pieced together this hidden timeline in Ep61. We obtain the ammunition in Ep-CHIP.b, and detonate it in Ep61.
★ The cause of Edmund's illness is not revealed in the memory chapters of Episodes 22-24. We only know it as a "chronic disease".

Autumn 12 ★ Turning Point: Edmund begins to decline.
The world progresses from the sky to the ground, then to the workshop, and finally to the bedside. [Collapse begins]
Anna was forced to take over Edmund's mechanical work—not because she wanted to be a mechanic.
It's because those things need to be done by someone, and Edmund can't do them anymore.
—The world is starting to shut down.

At the age of 12, Anna became a de facto apprentice mechanic.
She used the perception Edmund taught her to take over his machine—the same kind of hearing.
But context has transformed from "the joy of discovery" into "the weight of responsibility".
At the same time, her perception has a cruel dual function:
She could hear Edmund's body weakening (the trembling in his hands began with his voice).
You can also hear his machine waiting for someone to take over.
The world remains closed. Flight vanishes. Nature vanishes. Only the workshop and the bedside remain.

Edmund died in the spring of his 13th year. A period of stagnation. He put on his red jacket.
Anna walks Edmund's route every day to do Edmund's work.
It's not because she was ready. It's because she couldn't stop.

The Lia incident at age 13. Six months of pent-up emotions erupted.
—The world shattered.
```

**The "World Closed" contrast function:**

Before (ages 9-12) | After (ages 12-13, Edmund's Decline) |
|---|---|
| Flight, Sky, Distant Places | Workshop, Bedside, Boardwalk |
| Perception = the joy of discovery | Perception = the pain of loss (hearing Edmund's decline) |
Edmund was happy alongside them. | Edmund was under the blanket, his hands hidden.
| Machines are "living things with tempers" (15%) | Machines are "responsibilities that require human intervention" (rising sharply) |
| World's largest | World's smallest |

Because Anna was taking care of Edmund, she no longer had time to go outside and connect with nature. Her sensory channels were still there—but the input had changed from "wind/water/sky/distant places" to "workshop/pipes/Edmund's breathing." Her sensory abilities hadn't disappeared, but the objects of her perception had narrowed.

The narrowing itself is painful: a 12-year-old girl who saw something that Edmund hadn't noticed (M-18) from 3,000 meters in the air just the previous year is now trapped on a 50-meter walkway between the workshop and her bed.

**Writing Constraints (#13 World Closes Contrast):** This phase is deployed in Ep24 (Scenes 1-2). It doesn't need to be written as a separate full chapter. However, the Ch.VIII opening in intro_arc needs to imply that this phase existed—Anna's role in the workshop wasn't routine; she had only been in charge of the space for six months, and her relationship with it was still new, unnatural, and carried Edmund's shadow. The mechanical takeover is placed in the **background**, not as the narrative focus. The focus remains on the shrinking relationship between Anna and Edmund. She learned to tighten bolts not because of the course schedule, but because Edmund's hands were shaking.

### Newly Opened Channel

| Channel | Content | Representative Scenarios |
|------|------|---------|
| Understanding System Relationships | "What you hear is not things—you hear the relationships between things" | M-20b voice-only |
| The Perception of Physical Decline | Edmund's Trembling Hands—Starting with a Subtle Change in His Voice | M-19 |
| Temperature disappears | Hands clasped together. Hand temperature disappears. | M-21 |
| Idling | Habitually preparing to speak → No one is listening → That pause | M-22 |

Why does perception change at this stage?

Edmund's last words to Anna during her illness—"the relationship between things"—were the final driving force behind the upgrade of Anna's perceptual system from "hearing a thing" to "hearing the system." But the emotional context for this upgrade was **loss**.

Edmund was covered with a blanket, his hands underneath. The teaching medium progressed from hand-in-hand to full-body perception to pure sound. **Contraction and growth are opposite.** This wasn't a "more advanced teaching method"—it was that Edmund's body no longer allowed him to teach with his hands.

Therefore, Anna's systemic perception ability was imbued with an underlying quality from the very first day of her life: **this ability was gained in exchange for her father's disappearance**. This wasn't something Anna would explicitly think about—it was a deep-seated structure of her bodily memory. Every time she "heard the relationship between things," her body simultaneously remembered the hand hidden under the blanket.

### The Red Jacket's Psychological Turning Point Timeline (Compiled Version, Updated on P-10)

```
Anna, 9 years old, was transporting Axiom parts with Edmund. She was unaware of the radiation.
[Hidden layer: Anna and we don't know]

When Anna was 9-10 years old, Edmund learned about radiation exposure and began preparing for Anna's future.
[Hidden Layer]

Anna, 10 years old, M-16, red jacket.
Anna's layer: Made by Dad. Dyed with red clay. Warm.
Edmund's layer (revealed in Ep61): He found out. It's red because it's visible from afar.

When Edmund was 10-12 years old, he traveled throughout the settlement weaving nets, sometimes with Anna in tow. Layers 2-3 were welded.
Anna's memory is a blurry composite—she can't tell which time she went where.
Anna's layer: Pride. Everyone knows Dad.
Edmund's layer (revealed in Episode 61): Weaving a web. Time is counting down. The web's scale far exceeds the area Anna is present in.

12-year-old Edmund decline. Layer 4 begins. The red jacket goes from "wear it out" to "wear it for safety".

Edmund died at age 13 in the spring. His red jacket, Layer 5, was welded shut.
Anna started wearing it and walking Edmund's route. Continuous pattern.

13 years old, spring to summer, half a year of idling. Don't cry. Keep going. Don't let anyone see inside.
(Before the Lia incident) Red jacket = Edmund is still here = the world is still turning = I'm doing okay.
The spring is taut.

Summer, age 13 ★ The Lia Incident. Wrench. The spring broke.
Trauma anchor point = close contact. Not clothing.
The perceived direction shifts from near distance to far distance.

13 years old, Summer to Autumn: Joren's work scope is adjusted. Anna assists in the team.
Heavy tool Flinch begins (body-level, irrational).
A three-second pause is established ("Was the hand that pushed me the wind or myself?").
He kept wearing the red jacket. No one questioned it. No one realized it either.
There was an invisible crack between the clothes and the hand.

Age 13, Autumn → Winter. Visiting Lia. Going empty-handed. "Try not." "Your jacket is so red."
Lia saw the red jacket—but she only saw the color.
Not anything behind the clothes.
The seven-year-old didn't remember Guilt. He remembered the brightest red in the gray and white room.

14 years old, spring. Received a work order from a settlement on the west coast.
★ Anna's gaze path: work order → tool wall → move away → outside the window → sky.
For the first time, I realized that leaving this workshop was a relief.
He wore a red jacket on the airship.
From then on, it began to exist in a place it was not designed to be.

14 years old - takeoff. Shoulders fall. Hands rest on the railing - loosely.
A railing isn't a wrench. It won't fly off. It's just there to keep you from falling.
The red jacket fluttered in the wind—in the sky, it wasn't a Guardian's mark.
It was just the color on a girl's body.

For individuals aged 14-19, mechanic frequency decreases, while flight frequency increases.
The red jacket was always on him.
Anna is wearing clothes that are close to the ground but doing things that are far away.
The contradiction remained unseen by anyone, including herself.

19 years old (Arc 1) We met Anna for the first time:
A pilot, wearing a mechanic-colored jacket.
I have a vague feeling that something is wrong. I can't quite put my finger on it.
```

### How to write code on the machine at this stage

At this stage, machines are vessels of sorrow, not objects of technology.

Edmund teaches Anna to understand "relationships between things"—the key point is that this is **the last thing he teaches her**, not how amazing this ability is.
Anna was fixing something during a lull in the process—the fixing was mechanical and unconscious, and when she finished and looked up, she realized there was no one to share it with—that pause was the heart of the scene.
- Gyroscope pen holder, high stool—these ingenious tools Edmund made during his decline, the pain stemming from the idea that "the better the tool, the more accurate the diagnosis"—he knew he was leaving, and he was equipping her.

**Never write, "Anna continued to refine her auditory diagnostic skills during Edmund's illness."** She wasn't refining them. She was losing someone. What she learned were fragments of the process of loss, not achievements.

**Emotional keywords for this stage:** contraction, final, loss of warmth, pause, idling.

]

## I. How does the door open?

Approximately eight months after the Lia incident.

Anna's days took on a new rhythm. Joren adjusted her work scope—she assisted his maintenance team, no longer handling large equipment alone. This wasn't punishment. It was a reasonable arrangement, one that Anna herself agreed to. Her hands could still work, but something new had grown between them—a hesitation she couldn't yet name. A three-second pause had begun to form within her; each time the pressure in her hands approached a certain threshold, she would briefly pull back from the action.

That early spring, a coastal settlement on the West Coast needed to ship a shipment from Atheria—breeding starter cultures from Canyon, several boxes of copper leaf seedlings cultivated in the Atheria valley's greenhouse, and a replacement valve set made by Joren. It was a standard freight schedule. Joren had originally sent someone else, but that person had a family emergency. They were short-handed to accompany the shipment.

"Send Anna," Joren said to the scheduling person. "She knows the specifications of that valve assembly; she can help with the installation when she gets there."

No one hesitated. It was a very small task.

There was only one problem: there was no road between Atheria and the settlement. It was separated by a river valley carved into the central plain and dozens of kilometers of hills. The only way was by airship.

Had Anna ever ridden in an airship before? She flew with Edmund when she was a child. But in the year following the Lia incident, she didn't leave Atheria. Her world shrank to the fifty-meter boardwalk between the Eagle's Nest workshop and the valley floor.

That morning, when she arrived at the airport with a small bag on her back—not a tool bag, just the manifest needed for the cargo and a few changes of clothes—a Class II cargo airship awaited her. The captain's name was Hagen. His brown face was covered in fine lines from the wind, he had a long scar on his arm, and his beard looked like a used steel wool pad.

"From Freedman's?"

"yes."

"Does your mom know you're here?"

"She signed it."

Are you seasick?

"have no idea."

Hagen's eyebrows twitched. "Okay. Get on. Don't throw up on my deck."

---

Anna stood by the starboard railing during takeoff. Hagen told her to stand there—"New pilots always want to look outside the first time, and it's less windy over there."

One by one, the mooring lines were untied. The airship rose from its berth like a bubble from the seabed. Quietly. Anna's stomach sank, then settled. The airport platform shrank beneath her feet. The two cliffs of the Atheria Valley receded to either side like two doors opening.

Then--

world.

This wasn't a bird's-eye view of the basin—the one she'd seen countless times from the Eagle's Nest railing. It was a world beyond the basin. The central plains unfolded towards the canyon mouth, with golden-green vibratoforests spreading along the horizon, shimmering in the wind, the whole land seeming to breathe. Further away, the skyline of Frontier City loomed in the morning mist—old-fashioned domes and towers, enveloped by five-hundred-year-old growth layers, looked from this distance like an ancient, moss-covered mountain range.

Anna gripped the railing tightly. Not out of fear, but because it was too big.

"It's always like this the first time," Hagen said from the cockpit. The wind muffled his voice, but she could hear him laughing. "You won't feel so big anymore once you've flown enough."

"I don't want to fly enough," Anna said.

Hagen didn't answer. But he later mentioned the trip to others: "Most beginners, on their first flight, react by gripping tighter and pulling back. The Freedman girl let go and leaned forward."

---

## Part Two: Three Days on the Coast

The journey to the coastal settlement wasn't long—a little over three hours. Anna stood on the deck most of the time. Hagen didn't let her sit idle—there were no "passengers" on the airship, only "crew members who hadn't yet learned how to work." He had her help with hauling in the cables, passing tools, and checking the barometer. Anna did it. Her hands felt a strange ease as she performed these unfamiliar tasks—because the cables weren't wrenches. The cables wouldn't fly off and hit anyone. They were only there to hold things in place.

That place is completely different from Atheria.

There are no canyons. No cliffs. It sits on gentle hills facing Azure Main. The air is salty and damp with earth. The fields stretch to the horizon—not the meticulously planned terraces at the bottom of canyons like those in Atheria, but vast, luxuriously flat farmland, the wheat ears swaying like golden waves in the sea breeze. The airport isn't on the cliff face but atop a grassy slope, the airships moored to anchor piles on the ground like a herd of giant buffalo grazing in the meadow.

**The first thing that struck her was the sound.**

It's not quiet—it has its own voice. But it lacks the echo of a canyon. In Atheria, every sound bounces off the cliffs, creating reverberation and layering, resulting in a three-dimensional, rich sound. Here, the sound is flat. It dissipates as soon as it leaves the ground, like water splashed on sand.

Anna's sound map almost completely fails here.

It took her most of the day to adjust. By the next morning, she could begin to read the chords of this place—not the dense symphony of Atheria, but a more expansive, wind- and sea-based plains soundscape. The direction of the wheat field's waves indicated the wind direction. The speed of the waves indicated the wind speed. The occasional "still bands" in the waves indicated that the wheat was shorter than the surrounding area, possibly due to lack of water or soil problems.

No one taught her these things. This was an extension of Atheria's perception that grew out of itself in a completely different environment.

The valve assembly was quickly settled—locals took over the installation, Anna helped check the specifications, and confirmed everything was in order. She spent the rest of her time walking around the settlement. Not working. Just walking.

She walked through wheat fields swaying in the sea breeze. She passed through a market completely different from the ones in Atheria—flatter, more spread out, with stalls spaced further apart, because here there was no need for the crowds of the canyon. She smelled flour, dried grass, and the salty, fishy aroma of seaweed baked by the sun in the distance. In the communal dining hall, she ate something she'd never tasted before—a soft, fluffy bread made with local finely ground flour, dipped in a thick root soup.

An old woman who runs a bakery—everyone calls her "Aunt Flour" because she is always covered in a layer of white flour from head to toe—serves this bowl of soup to her without saying a word.

"Thank you... but I have nothing to offer—"

"I can use the starter culture you brought from Atheria tomorrow. The bread you ate was made from last year's batch." Aunt Flour's tone was as if she were commenting on the nice weather. "Don't be shy. Eat."

That evening, as Anna helped Aunt Flour move the canyon starter culture into the cool storage room, she sat on the grassy slope of the airport. Azure Main, bathed in the twilight light, became a plane of deep blue and orange-gold. Above, there were no canyon walls, only the unobstructed sky. Betelgeuse stretched out to the west, much larger than it appeared from within the canyon—unbound by cliffs, it filled half the sky. Rivers of purple and rose light stretched from the horizon to the zenith.

**Different places have different smells.** Atheria smells of rocks, metal, and engine oil. Here, it smells of flour, salty winds, and dried grass. The people in these two places live on the same planet, speak the same language, and eat the same wheat introduced by terraform—but their daily lives are two completely different sensory worlds.

This is what the pilot sees. Not from a fixed point—but moving between different points, each stop offering a different taste, different light, and a different person bringing you a bowl of soup.

That evening, Anna made a decision. She didn't know how to fly an airship yet, but she knew she had to learn.

---

## III. Learning to Fly

### Hagen's Rules

After returning to Atheria, Anna went to find Hagen.

"Teach me to fly."

Hagen was repairing the worn sails on deck. He looked up at her.

"How old are you?"

"fourteen."

"The official document will be submitted on the 15th."

"I can learn first."

What did your mom say?

"She said she has no objection if you're willing to teach."

Hagen stood up. He was a head and a half taller than her. He held up three fingers.

"My rules. First, I say stop, and you stop. Whether you think you're right or wrong. You can argue afterward—I like arguing with people—but in the air, what I say is the law."

"Second, if you're seasick, wipe yourself off. Then get back to work."

"Third," his expression turned serious. "An airship isn't a workshop. In a workshop, if you make a mistake, the worst that can happen is a pump explodes or a wall collapses. On an airship, if you make a mistake, you and everyone on board will fall from two thousand meters. I've heard about the mistakes you've made on land. I don't care. That's a land matter. But on my airship, think for three seconds before you make a mistake: Will your decision cause someone else to fall? Think it through before you act. If you're not sure, come ask me."

Anna nodded.

"Sunrise tomorrow. Airport berth B3. Bring your own gloves."

[Design Note] Hagen's "three-second" coincidence—he was unaware that Anna had already paused for three seconds. But his "think for three seconds" and Anna's "pause for three seconds to ask herself about the source of her motivation" are isomorphic at the body level. One stems from trauma, the other from the practical needs of flight safety. These two three-second pauses from different sources overlap in Anna's case—the product of trauma gains a professional context, becoming a dual foundation. This overlap doesn't need to be spoken aloud by anyone.

### From Deck to Steer

For the first three months, Anna didn't touch the steering wheel. She did deck work: taking up lines, checking airbags, repairing sails, and monitoring temperatures. Hagen's way of teaching flight was strikingly similar to Edmund's way of teaching perception—first letting you feel with your hands, then telling you the name of the principle.

Anna's hands quickly developed new calluses—not the kind from tightening bolts in the workshop, but from the friction of thick cables. She learned to maintain her balance in the wind. The airship's deck tilted and swayed slightly in the air currents, like standing on a live fish. For the first two weeks, she walked like she was drunk.

But she learned faster than most beginners. Not because of mechanic skills—it was because her body already had nine years of sensory foundation. She could "hear" the way the wind blew through the deck trusses—winds from different directions creating different frequency patterns within the metal structure. She could feel the changes in airbag pressure on her skin—tiny gradients in temperature and humidity. She knew the deck's tendency to tilt—it was the same balance system she'd used for over a decade walking on the Atheria cliff walkway, only the frame of reference was changing.

Hagen thought he was teaching a novice. He was actually giving someone with nine years of sensory experience a new domain. Not a mechanical one. It was nine years of experience of hearing, seeing, and living in the world with one's body.

Three months later, on a calm afternoon—a standard flight, with clear skies—Hagen called her over.

"Sit there. Hold on."

The steering wheel felt alive in her hands. A constant, slight tremor—a combined feedback from the wind, airbags, and hull structure transmitted through the control cables. Not the pulse of a machine. It was the symphony of the entire ship, the air around it, and the earth beneath it—all of these things.

"You felt it?" Hagen asked from the side.

"...It's talking to me."

"say what?"

Anna closed her eyes. The wind was coming from the left front, at an angle of about twenty degrees. The hull had a slight tendency to roll to the right. The rudder response was gentle, without any sudden changes.

"Everything is normal. But the port side is drifting a little bit to starboard. One or two degrees."

Hagen was silent for five seconds. Then he said something Anna remembered for a long time:

"Your dad taught you to understand machines. I'll teach you to understand the sky. Once you understand both languages, you'll be complete."

[Design Note] Hagen's statement carries retroactive weight after its climax in Episode 61—Edmund's teaching of "listening" and Hagen's teaching of "listening" represent the same perceptual system expressed in two different domains. Anna's choice to fly doesn't mean abandoning what Edmund gave her—it means taking what Edmund gave her to the skies. However, this understanding is only conveyed to us in Episode 25 through the present-time flight scene, not explicitly stated in the backstory.

That afternoon, with Hagen's hand hovering over hers—not touching, just there like a safety net—Anna took her first flight. Forty minutes. Straight line. Smooth.

Her hands trembled as she handed the steering wheel back to Hagen. Not from nervousness. It was something deeper than joy. As a child, when flying kites, she had felt the wind as a dance partner. Now she knew that was just the prelude.

### Assessment

One month after my fifteenth birthday. Formal pilot apprenticeship assessment.

Archeon has no flight school. A civilization of one million people doesn't have the extra resources to maintain formal educational institutions. Pilot training relies entirely on an apprenticeship system—you fly enough hours with an experienced pilot, learn enough skills, and once your mentor deems you ready, he takes you to take the exam.

The assessment takes place in Frontier City—where there are the most pilots, the most complete airport facilities, and an informal judging panel called "The Murder." Six or seven veteran pilots with over twenty years of flying experience sit in the airport teahouse every day, drinking tea and commenting on every airship that arrives or departs. There are no uniforms, no documents, no offices. But in The Loom, if "The Murder" nods, you're a qualified pilot. If they shake their heads, no captain will hire you.

The assessment was a real flight. Real route, real cargo, real weather.

Anna's assigned route that day was to a medium-sized settlement called Iron Vine—located in a deep valley on the edge of the central plain. Approaching required precise, low-speed turns in the narrow valley to align with the berth. The "crow" sent to observe was an elderly woman named Savona—in her sixties, with a full head of silver hair and skin as black as if it had been smoked. It was said that in her youth, she was the only person to survive flying backwards out of the Serpent's Tooth Falcon Gate storm. She sat in the corner of the cabin without uttering a single word from beginning to end.

The valley bend was crucial. The crosswind was stronger than expected. Anna completed a 90-degree turn in less than 500 meters, controlling the descent rate and dealing with the accelerating crosswinds. Her hands remained steady on the steering wheel—she wasn't thinking about theory, she was thinking about the kite. As a child, flying a kite in the updrafts of the canyon, the string taut in her hand, the instant the wind changed direction wasn't a reaction of the brain, but of the hand.

Docked. Smooth and precise.

Savona stood up. She walked over. Without saying a word, she extended her root-like hand, patted Anna on the shoulder, and turned away.

Hagen leaned closer and whispered, "She took your picture. That means you passed."

Anna took three seconds to understand the sentence. Then her knees buckled. She sat on the deck, looked up at the narrow strip of sky above the Iron Vine Valley, and smiled.

---

## IV. Days on the Flight (Ages 16-18)

### The world is a sensory map

After passing the assessment, Anna's world transformed from a canyon into a net.

She flew most of the routes as co-pilot. The western route was easy—plain terrain and steady westerly winds. The southern route crossed the northern edge of the Orun Plateau—the airflow was like a pot of boiling water. The eastern hilly region was a different story—remote settlements with only a few hundred people, and berths were just two stakes and a rope in the grass.

Each settlement was a different sensory experience. Anna began to build a map within her body—not a flight path, but a **taste map**.

**Coppergate**'s first impression is dryness. The air carries a sun-baked, dusty smell—a thin layer of ochre-red dust carried by the wind from the Orun Plateau to the south, settling in this gateway town for north-south trade. Cargo density is higher than elsewhere—it's not uncommon to see three or four airships docked at the airport simultaneously. The noise is deafening, but efficient; the loading and unloading pace is as rapid as drumming. Anna tastes salted jerky brought from further south for the first time here—it's so salty she drinks three cups of water, but it has a smoky, mineral flavor she's never experienced in Atheria.

**A big city** is about density. Anna understood for the first time what a "big city" meant—not size, but the density of choices. In Atheria, you only have two or three options for lunch. Here, you can stand in the center of the market and see fifteen different kinds of food steaming hot. The layers of sounds kept her up all night.

The small outposts in the eastern hills are quiet and vast. Sounds scatter across the grasslands in a way similar to coastal settlements, but drier—lacking the low-frequency undertones of the sea, the distant wind is the baseline. Some outposts have only a few dozen households. The arrival of an airship is an event. Everyone comes out to watch. Children chase after its shadow. Anna stays in these places for two or three days, not because of a lot of work—but because the flight intervals are that long. While waiting for her return flight, she eats and chats with the locals, is taken to see newborn horned lambs, and is given a jar of homemade brew by an old woman.


### The prototype of the shuttle man

What does Anna do on the flight path?

**Transporting goods.** The most basic thing. The Gustress carries cargo. But the cargo isn't abstract cargo—it's a barrel of salted fish from a small town in the southern canyon to Atheria, a basket of fresh seafood from the West Coast, a letter, a packet of seeds, and a repaired tool brought back from a small outpost request. Each item connects to a person and a need. Anna knows who salted the fish. Who the seeds are for. She is the human bridge between these goods.

**Bringing Messages.** Airships arrive carrying two things: cargo and news. Anna arrives at a settlement, bringing news from three places: who had a baby; which flight path had been affected by recent strong winds and schedule changes; the Thread Festival being moved up two weeks this year; and the poor yellowhead harvest on the Iron Vine. This news was as valuable as the cargo—a council might adjust its food reserves based on a casual remark from Anna.

**Look.** This is Anna's unique value. She arrives at a settlement, takes a stroll. No rush. Not looking for faults—she's sensing the rhythm. The canal flows faster than last time; it's rained upstream. The market is quieter; something's affecting trade. People's faces are either tighter or looser. The stone bridge feels half a degree warmer underfoot—in short, without pausing: "Spring is deepening." Then she continues walking.

She carried these observations to the next stop. Not in the form of a report—but casually mentioned over a long-table lunch. "Coppergate has had a lot of salt transiting through this year, and they'd like to swap some of your Canyon brews for it. Interested?" "The irrigation at that little outpost on the east side seems to be having some problems. If anyone goes there next time, could you check it out?" This casual flow of information was the lifeblood of The Loom.

She wasn't just reading the water and the wind. Once, while docked at Coppergate, Anna noticed a pottery vendor at the dockside market had moved his stall three steps further inward from where he'd last been. She didn't stop. But as she walked, her body began to adjust: the dockworkers were wearing thicker clothes than before; the tent ropes across the square were tauter than usual; an old man mending fishing nets in the harbor wasn't sitting in his usual spot—he'd moved to the sheltered corner against the wall. Three people, three subtle adjustments in behavior, all pointing to the same thing. After returning to the Gustatory, Anna adjusted the departure time for the next day. She didn't explain why to anyone. The weather changed in the early hours of the next morning.

[Design Notes] This beat is a living extension of the M-08's "Birds Know Before the Sky." Miriam's "reading living things" and Edmund's "reading dead things" are two parallel lines. In the flying era, Edmund's line was fully extended (rudder wheel sensing, airbag pressure, sound maps), and Miriam's line here adds a prose layer—Anna doesn't just read bird behavior to predict the weather; she reads human behavior patterns. The "snapshot of the old man's eye" in the M-12 market day when he was nine years old was unconscious; the same set of perceptions at seventeen had been internalized into work habits. Joren later said, "You look at the water, the wind, people, and their expressions"—this prose is evidence of that statement.

Does Anna not fix things? Yes, she does. But fixing things is never her reason for going to a place—it's something she happens to encounter while she's in a place. It's like helping someone who has fallen down on the street—helping someone isn't your purpose in going out, but you did do it.

[Design Constraints] Anna's mechanic work in settlements (ages 16-18) should account for ≤10% of screen time. If a prose sequence spends more than 3 sentences describing her crouching down to fix something, that's too much. Repairing is a node in the transit, not the destination. Her narrative rhythm should be: fly—land—walk—look—speak—fly. Not: fly—land—crouch—repair—repair—repair—fly.

### Fork in the Orun South Line (16 years old)

That early winter, Anna and Hagen flew an emergency transport mission.

In a small settlement called Stone Bridge in the Orun Plateau canyon—three hundred people—the fish-salting season has arrived, but they're short of salt. Salt is transported from the north, the fastest route cutting directly across the northern edge of the Orun Plateau. The salt shortage isn't a survival crisis—but three thousand fish are waiting on racks for salt, and in three more days they'll have to be thrown into the river. A crucial source of protein for the entire settlement year. Hagen takes the job. Anna drives.

As we climbed past the central plains and up to the northern edge of Orun, problems arose. The predicted disturbance was much wider than it actually was—a dark gray wall of clouds stretched across the flight path, its base churning with tearing textures of shear airflow.

"The air pressure is dropping," Anna said. "Faster than predicted."

Hagen said nothing. His hands were on his knees. He was waiting for her to make a decision—about the teaching method for the last six months.

Two options. Go through—she can manage, the shortest total flight time, and the fastest way to get the salt. Take the 60-kilometer detour—much safer, but an extra hour and a half.

An hour and a half. Three thousand fish are waiting at Stone Bridge.

Anna's hand gripped the steering wheel. She would get through. She could get through. She wasn't a novice.

Then she felt it—the tightness in her chest.

It wasn't fear. It wasn't excitement. It was a push that was more familiar than either. "Someone's waiting, and I can't keep them waiting any longer." "The Freedmans are reliable." "What will Hagen think if I take the detour?"—even though she knew Hagen would never look down on her for choosing the safe route.

These thoughts didn't come in a queue. They were all mixed together, pushing from the belly all the way to the fingertips. The force said: Go through it. You can do it.

Anna recognized the shape of the thrust.

Not entirely clear—she was sixteen, not forty. But she recognized it. She'd seen the same shape next to the water pump three years ago. That time, it wore the cloak of "professional judgment," beneath which lay "I need to prove myself."

She wasn't sure if it was wearing the same thing this time. Maybe wearing it before really was the right thing to do. She couldn't quite figure it out in this moment—her hands on the steering wheel, the cloud wall looming ahead.

But she can do one thing: when things are uncertain, she leaves room for flexibility.

"bypass."

Hagen nodded slightly. His chin moved only two millimeters.

The journey outside wasn't entirely calm—the marginal airflow was angular, and the airship glided through the crosswinds like a person walking on gravel—but there was no danger. We arrived at Stone Bridge an hour and ten minutes late. The salt had been unloaded. The fish were saved.

That night, Anna sat on the deck with her feet dangling over the side. The dark silhouette of the Orun Plateau resembled a sleeping behemoth in the starlight.

She didn't know if her decision was right. What if she had made it through? Maybe nothing would have happened. Maybe she would have saved an hour and a half. Maybe someone would have avoided the wait. This was another thing she was learning: most choices don't offer a "right answer." If you choose the safe route, you never know what the other path will hold. You can only keep flying with uncertainty.

Hagen was also on deck that night. The two remained silent for a long time. Then he spoke—not as an evaluation, but to tell his own story.

"When I was twenty-five, I worked at Orun's South Edge Express. Someone was urgently waiting. There was a wall of clouds in front of me. I passed through it."

Anna looked at him.

"Did they go through it?"

"It made it through. It's alright." His expression was obscured by the starlight. "But that night I sat there until dawn, my hands shaking the whole time. Not because of the bumps. It was because when I made that decision, I wasn't thinking about wind speed or structural stress. I was thinking about how the recipient would react if they got impatient."

He poured the cold tea into the darkness below the deck.

"You went around in circles today. Maybe you went the wrong way. Maybe you should wear it next time. The key isn't whether you went around in circles or wore it—the key is that when you made the decision, you knew whether the hand pushing you was the wind or yourself. You knew that today. That's more valuable than arriving an hour and a half early."

Anna sat on the deck for a while longer. The lights of Stone Bridge looked like a handful of fireflies scattered in the dark valley.

She remembered Miriam's words from three years ago—"You've been pretending you're okay for six months." Those words, spoken when she was thirteen, were a heavy blow. Now she began to understand: recognizing one's own state wasn't a one-time realization, but a practice, and the kind of practice that could never be perfected in a lifetime.

[Design Note] "Is the hand that pushes you the wind or yourself?"—Hagen is unaware that he's repeating something Anna has already been doing (three-second pause). But he gives it a flying metaphor, imbuing Anna's traumatic product with professional dignity. The weight this line carries is different when it reappears in the present-time race in Ep25—it has been used and tested for six years by then.

### Rafale

Anna is seventeen years old. She discovers a Class II airship that has been declared scrapped in an abandoned berth in Frontier City.

The airbags are leaking, the transmission is rusted, three control cables are broken, and there are more patches on the body panels than on the original parts. The previous owner parked it here and never came back.

Anna crouched down and tapped the keel. The sound was deep and steady, without any hollowness. She touched the main structural beam—nano-reinforced concrete from the old era, rusted on the surface but intact inside.

"The skeleton is fine," she told Hagen. "I can fix everything else."

"You're crazy."

She traded three months of free repair work—maintenance of public facilities at Frontier Airport—for ownership. Then it took four months to fix. It wasn't done by one person—Joren sent parts from Atheria, Hagen's crew came to help on their days off, and several people Anna had helped in other settlements before also came to lend a hand.

She named it "Gust." During its first test flight, a sudden gust of wind blew a loosely secured toolbox off the deck, sending it crashing into a sweet potato stall tent below. The stall owner poked his head out of the tent and shouted into the sky, "That gust of wind almost killed me!"

Anna lost the money for two sweet potatoes.

During the four months of ship repairs, Anna heard the port's steam whistles and organ almost every day. As the steam rose in the evening, the musicians would begin operating the wall-sized array of pipes—the low-frequency foghorns would come first, followed by layers of mid-range and high-range notes. She now knew the relationship between the diameter and pitch of each pipe, and which valves combined to create which chords. But on some evenings, when the wind was just right, the sound would travel from the direction of the port, and her hand, tightening bolts on the deck, would pause for a second—not long—and the phrase from when she was eight would surface, not in words, but as a feeling of lightness in her chest. Someone wanted the wind to sing. The wind agreed.

[Design Notes] M-09 callback. The weight difference in a single sentence: At eight, "someone wanted the wind to sing" was pure wonder—she didn't know what instrument it was; at seventeen, she fully understood the principles of physics, but that initial wonder remained in the form of a bodily sensation. Knowing "how it was done" without killing "that's beautiful."

### go home

Anna doesn't return to Atheria every time she goes. There's a window of opportunity about every three or four weeks—three to five days.

The routine on the way back is always the same: first, stand at a high point in Skyport for a while.

It wasn't a ritual. More like a bodily habit. After flying back for weeks, she needed to hear Atheria's chords again before stepping into the workshop. In those weeks, the basin's sounds had subtly changed—perhaps the winch on the elevator had finally had its bearings replaced, the pulse intervals shortened, perhaps the river had risen, thickening the low frequencies, perhaps the Skyport tower under construction had grown taller, altering the echo angle. Two or three minutes, letting the new baseline cover the old. Then down. Then a hot meal—after weeks of hard bread and dried meat, anything with gravy made her squint with happiness. Then home.

Miriam never waited for her at the airport. It wasn't that she didn't care—she was busy herself. She was the community's logistical backbone, coordinating supplies, keeping inventory records, and connecting people. Anna's hands were maintaining the hardware; Miriam's hands were maintaining the software.

Their conversation was brief but precise. Anna put down her bag, washed her hands, and sat down at the table. Miriam brought her tea. It was always the same—brewed from dried leaves from the valley's greenhouse, a slightly bitter, burnt flavor with a hint of sweetness. Then came the question: "How was the trip?"

Anna said. It wasn't a heroic tale—it was a work report and observations from the journey. Miriam listened, occasionally asking not about technical details, but rather, "How are the people over there?"

Miriam started stuffing things into Anna's flight bag. Not every time. Anna would pack her bag before departure—manifest, change of clothes—and when she opened it the next day on the airship, there would sometimes be an extra item: a bag of dried berries, a pair of thick socks, a small roll of bandages, a bottle of wildflower oil. No notes. No explanations.

Those socks were worn until they were torn before being mended. Those berries were eaten when most needed. That bottle of wildflower oil was always in the side pocket of my flight bag, and by the time I was nineteen, I had used half of it.

Anna never said thank you to these things in person. Nor did she ever take them out.

[Design Note] The Miriam storyline is quiet but persistent during this period. Everything she stuffs into Anna's flight bag is a variation of "there's someone on the other side of the road when you fix something": someone is waiting for you to return from the other side of the sky you fly through. These items don't need to appear in Ep23-24. But if in Ep66 (the final scene of Act 1) or later in Arc, Anna at some moment pulls something from her flight bag that she didn't put there, we'll know who it is.

Joren's words

One day, between the ages of seventeen and eighteen, Anna returned from a trip to the Far Eastern hills. She was working with Joren at the Atheria workshop. She recounted her experiences from the trip—a problem had occurred at an outpost irrigation branch, and she had relayed this information to another settlement along the way, asking them to take a look.

Joren listened for a while and then fell silent.

"What you're doing isn't what a mechanic does."

Anna waited.

"A mechanic goes to a place, sees what's broken, fixes it, and leaves. You go to a place, stand still for ages—observing the water, the wind, the people, their expressions—and then tell them, 'Your real problem isn't that valve, it's the upstream water distribution.' That's not a mechanic. That's—" He paused. "I don't know what to call it. A lookout? Your dad was like that too. But you see further than him. He looked at a basin. You look at the relationships between several settlements."

Anna did not answer.

Joren mentioned something else—a real limitation.

"But you have one flaw. You can't walk."

It's not a security issue. It's something else.

"You arrive at a place, encounter a problem, and you don't leave. Not because it can't be fixed—it's because you feel there's something more to do. You feel that if you leave, that place will be missing something. You have to learn something new—'good enough.' Not perfect. You have to learn to draw a line between 'it's good enough' and 'I want to see how it goes,' and then stand on this side of the line and move on. Because every extra day you stay in one place is an extra day waiting in another."

Anna thought about it for a long time. Later, she did try to adjust. But it was harder than learning the three-second pause—because when she left a place, she always felt something was left undone. Not just something unfinished. It was a deeper unease: what if the person in red left and no one stepped in?

There's something deeper. Not a thought—it's a location. The workshop entrance. The doorway I pass every morning when I put on my red jacket and head out. If no one's standing there—

She pushed that flashback back. It doesn't belong here.

Joren was talking about work habits. He didn't realize he was also hitting a nerve with something else.

[Design Note] "Won't leave" is an expression of the same pattern in M-25 at different scales. In M-25, Anna "doesn't stop working, doesn't leave the workshop" for six months after Edmund's death—she also "won't leave," not from the settlement, but from Edmund's path, from her role as a mechanic. Leaving = a break in continuity = a disconnect in Edmund's world. Joren's surface meaning is a matter of work efficiency, but he doesn't know where the roots of this pattern are. The prose uses a half-second body-level flash (at the workshop entrance) to activate both layers simultaneously, then they are covered back by reason—Anna, at seventeen, doesn't yet have the ability to parse this connection, but the reader can reconnect after obtaining M-25.

This became a real, ongoing daily struggle—not a major psychological trauma, but the oscillation of a young woman in a red jacket between "I can't go" and "I must go." At nineteen, she was a little better than she was at seventeen. But she knew it was still her most common mistake.

[Design Note] Joren's observation of "not walking" will be behaviorally expressed in Arc 2-3 through Anna's time spent in the settlements. Kassia may be the first to directly point out this pattern—in a completely different language. But that's a story for later.

### Eighteen-year-old Milo

Anna was 18 in early summer. She had been flying the Rafale solo for a year.

Milo came from Thornfield, a small outpost on Anna's flight route—a village of a few hundred people in the southwestern interior of the Basin, surrounded by hills and dry valleys. His family was unremarkable. His father was a local carpenter, and his mother worked as a cook in a common house. Milo was the third child in the family. He exhibited something Anna recognized: a desire to see the outside world.

It's not a drama. It's about a thirteen-year-old boy who, after helping Anna with routine delivery at Thornfield, stood on the deck of the Rafale, looking up at the Horizon, and asked, "What's the first thing you see after you take off from here?"

Anna hadn't considered taking on an apprentice. It was more likely Hagen's suggestion—"You've been flying alone for too long, take someone with you. Not for your own good, but so that someone can take over if something happens to you." Or it could be some kind of informal arrangement by Loom, where someone from Thornfield mentioned to Frontier that "a kid wants to go abroad," and the news got through a few hands before reaching Anna's ears.

When Milo boarded the ship on his first day, he brought an oversized dark brown short jacket—I don't know who gave it to him. He was talkative, but quick-witted. He learned well, but after completing a task, he would look at Anna's face to wait for her reaction—he lacked confidence. Anna was still getting used to something: needing to use language to convey things she had previously only operated at a physical level.

When Anna first tried to teach Milo to "listen" to the cable tension, she opened her mouth twice but couldn't find the right word. On the third try, she blurted out, "Put your hand on it and wait for it to tell you."

She paused for a second after saying that. These were Edmund's words. Not hers. She had never realized those words existed within her until the moment she needed to teach another person.

[Design Note] Milo's appearance activated the Edmund language remnant within Anna.
This is not a dramatic revelation—it's a regular ghost echo.
She discovered that her mouth would automatically choose Edmund's sentence structure when teaching.
Some she knew belonged to Edmund. Some she thought were hers—until one day Miriam overheard someone say, "Your dad said the same thing."
In Arc 1-3, this echo represents the background texture, not the plot point.

When Arc 1 started (Anna was 19), Milo had already been flying with her for six months to a year. The adjustment period was over. Their dynamic was: playful but with clear levels. Anna was relaxed in a safe context—she would joke, deliberately let Milo make mistakes to see how he handled them, and kick the toolbox over with her toe instead of handing it to him. When stakes increased, there was a switch—hand overriding, verbal override, "I'll do this." Milo didn't fully understand this switch. Neither did we.

[Design Note 1.5 Downpour Twist] Anna's protective action towards Milo as the stakes rise—one hand steadying her, body facing the source of the threat, steps neither quickening nor panicking—is structurally similar to Edmund's action of tucking her into his leather jacket in the downpour. This isn't something she "learned" from Edmund; it's a transmission of bodily memory. If this beat were incorporated into the episode prose (e.g., the sudden situation on deck), Anna's consciousness shouldn't conjure up the thought of "like her father"—only readers familiar with 1.5 would recognize this posture. The backstory doesn't plant the complete scene, leaving it to the episode writer to find that specific moment. But this note ensures the episode writer knows this connection exists and won't miss it.

[Kassia access point still TBD]
Kassia is also likely to appear around the 17-18 age window. Specific contact methods are yet to be determined.
Most likely, it was a routine encounter on the flight. Kassia's way of looking at Anna was different from the very first glance.
— That kind of weary recognition, like "yet another person wearing what they believe in."
Kassia's full access can be completed when writing Arc 2. Arc 1 does not need its origin.

---

## V. Before the age of nineteen—Anna at the beginning of the story

One spring morning, Anna flew back to Atheria from the southern route.

The Gust wind passed through the last hill. The canyon opened up ahead. Two cliffs stood like stone gates. Morning mist rose from the valley floor, tinged with gold by the sunlight. A corner of the valley floor fields shone, glowing green. Sounds surged up from the canyon's throat—the clanging of metal from the elevators, the hammering of the cliffside workshops, the tinkling of bells in the wind, the sound of the Ishkar River rising from the deepest depths. None of the sounds were identifiable to her. They were all mixed together—Atheria.

Docked. Mooring lines were thrown out and secured. Engines shut off. The deck went from alive to dead.

Waiting for her on the airport platform was a work order and a pot of cold tea. The work order was in Joren's handwriting—"Water pump shaft seal leak in area A6, not urgent but don't delay." The cold tea was left there by someone, and a note was attached to the pot: "Thank you for your hard work. Return the pot to the cafeteria."

Anna sipped her cold tea as she walked down into the valley. Along the way, she passed a square—the day before market day; people were setting up tents, unloading goods, and two children were being chased by their mother as they rode on a spiky-horned sheep. The auntie baking bread saw her and waved her flour-covered hands.

"Anna! When are you going to Millhaven? Bring me five pounds of their finely ground flour; I'll trade two loaves of bread for it!"

"Next week, maybe. Three."

"Two and a half."

"make a deal."

She walked across a stone arch bridge. She could feel the warmth of the stones beneath her feet—warmer than when she last came back; spring was deepening. In the distance, the Skyport tower under construction had grown taller, and people were welding at the top of the scaffolding, sparks falling from above like daytime shooting stars.

The water pump in section A6 was an old friend—it was recorded in Edmund's logbook. Anna squatted beside it, her palm against the casing. A buzzing vibration traveled up through her palm. Steady. But the shaft seal was definitely not positioned quite right—a very slight, fluid friction sensation.

Three-second pause. Self-check. How's my condition today? Not bad. A little tired after three days of flying, but it doesn't affect my judgment. Not a high-pressure operation. It's okay to proceed.

Half an hour. Clean and efficient. She suppressed the urge to adjust the support frame next to her as well—Joren's voice echoed in her mind: "Good enough. Let's go."

That's it for today. Stand up. Wipe your hands.

On my way home, I passed the Sunrise Rock. The shrubs of the sunberry bushes had just sprouted new leaves; it was still a long way from bearing fruit. A few clumps of blue hyacinths had already bloomed in the cracks of the rocks, small and swaying in the wind as if nodding.

Miriam wasn't home—probably coordinating something on the other side of the basin. The freshly brewed tea on the table was still steaming. Beside it was a note in Miriam's tight handwriting: "Tea in the pot. Rice in the pot. Don't use that cracked cup anymore. I got you a new one. Second shelf in the cupboard."

The new cup is made of ceramic. It's handmade. There's a small stain on the glaze. The feel is just right.

Anna carried the tea to the grassy area behind the workshop, where she could see the entire basin. She sat down.

The basin stretched out in the spring afternoon sun. Water pipes gleamed in the sunlight. In the distance, an airship was docking—from this distance, it was just a slow-moving, dark shape, like a fish swimming in golden air. Further on, the Frontier City skyline.

The wind blew in from the mouth of the canyon, carrying the scent of fresh grass and the distant smell of sparks from metalworking.

An ordinary afternoon. No crisis. No decisions.

Anna sat there, watching the world below turn. The elevator moved up and down the cliff face, like breathing. Some lights would come on at dusk. Some were built by her before. Some were built by others. Some were designed five hundred years ago by someone whose name she would never know, and they were still running.

I found a dried, sun-dried strawberry in my pocket—I don't know when it got there. I put it in my mouth. The sweetness slowly melted in my mouth.

This is the Anna Freedman we know.


Image 2 (rolling hills of a coastal settlement) – People are walking, not fixing things. Windmills are in the distance. Wheat fields. The sky is vast. The feeling this image evokes is of a place that breathes, not a place that needs repair.
Image 4 (Arch Bridge Market) – People walk by the water, there are boats. The buildings are massive, but the people are small. This is not a picture of "infrastructure needing maintenance." This is a picture of "people living in a constructed world."
Images 5, 6, 7, 8, and 9 (various city street scenes) – market tents, pedestrians, vines, and sunlight. The most striking element in each image is not the machines, but the density of human activity and the symbiosis between plants and buildings.
Image 10 (outpost on the grassland) – wildflowers, a windmill, a person walking on the road. The emotional register of this image is tranquility and vastness, not "that windmill needs maintenance."
Image 12 (Airship over the canyon) – This is a scene from the movement. It's not the destination, it's the passage.
These images are saying that Archeon's settlements are living, flavorful, and socially textured places. Anna flies to each place, and her experience should be the smells, people, light, and sounds of those places—not their list of problems.

What else can Anna do in Settlements besides fixing things?
Returning to The Loom's design—the shuttle is "the moving eyes on the loom." Timelapse says, "Airships bring gossip," and "All news is brought by people." The shuttle's social function is not that of a traveling repairman, but rather a connecting node.
What specific activities could be:
Transportation and delivery. The most basic things. Anna flies the Rafale to carry goods. But the goods aren't abstract "cargo"—they're concrete things: a barrel of salted fish from a small town in the southern canyons to Atheria, a box of dried seaweed from Tidemark on the West Coast, a replacement part for an old piece of equipment commissioned by a small outpost. Behind each shipment is a person and a need. Anna knows who salted the fish, and which canteen the seaweed is for. She is the human bridge between things.
News. When Anna arrives at a settlement, she brings more than just goods—she brings news from three settlements. Who had a baby, which flight schedules have been disrupted due to recent strong winds, and the Frontier Market's Thread Festival being moved up two weeks this year. This news is just as valuable as the goods. A settlement council might adjust its grain storage strategy based on Anna's report that "Iron Vine's yellowtail harvest is poor this year."
Environmental perception and reporting. This is Anna's unique value—not fixing things, but reading the state of a place. She goes to a settlement, walks around, and uses her full system of perception (listening, taught by Edmund; observing, taught by Miriam) to read how the place is now. Not diagnosing faults—it's perceiving the rhythm. The canal is flowing faster than last time, indicating it's rained upstream. The market is quieter than last time, indicating something is affecting trade. People's faces are more tense or more relaxed than last time. She takes these observations to the next settlement.
Trade coordination. There's no formal trade system in The Loom. The shuttles are de facto trade intermediaries.
